# Gateway release notes

What changes, release by release, for the people who call the gateway and
the people who run it: what changed, who notices, and what to do. Each entry
has an id, and [Proof](#proof) names the tests that show it. A behaviour with
no test is not listed here as a feature.

## Unreleased

Every upstream try is now recorded as an attempt with its own cost and
outcome. `model: "auto"` picks a model from the providers your key can
reach (N2). Keys, request metadata and
org-config saves are validated as the entries below describe. An org config's infrastructure
secrets (cache, A2A, audit, alerting and MCP credentials, and alert channel
URLs that carry a credential) are stored encrypted and shown to no reader
(F12, F22), and the model hub's provider keys,
custom-model credentials and dataset secrets are stored encrypted (F21). Stored objects, MCP sessions and
the response cache are scoped to the caller's organization. Checks on the
request read the input of every API (C35), and the
arguments of a request's tool calls, which a mask rewrites (C36);
prompt-injection reads a tool's result and the system prompt (C37); and the
checks on the request run before the response cache, so a cached answer is
served only to a request they pass (C38). Async requests
from organization API keys are refused (C16). One timeout ceiling,
`server.max_request_timeout`, now holds every request outside MCP, chat
completions included, while async jobs keep their own cap (C23); a default
request timeout above it stops start-up (U9). Deepgram's and Cartesia's
streaming speech APIs are relayed for internal keys, and for any caller
when request auth is off (N12). Managed models,
managed tools and the console pages that are not finished ship switched off
or hidden (see
[Present but switched off](#present-but-switched-off)). What this means on
FutureAGI's hosted gateway is in
[On FutureAGI's hosted gateway](#on-futureagis-hosted-gateway).

This release makes no speed claim. Its only performance measurements are
in-process benchmarks of the chat-completion handler with a mock upstream
on 127.0.0.1. They are not end-to-end latency, overhead or throughput, and
none of those was measured. On an n2d-standard-8 VM that ran only the
release gate's benchmark job, this release's
`BenchmarkChatCompletionNonStreaming` median is 248,921 ns per request
against 235,190 ns for dev 44285494be, the dev commit it merges, both
built with go1.26.10: 5.8% (about 14 µs) more. It allocates 311 objects
per request against dev's 304, within the release's pinned budget of at
most 7 more.
The figures, the machine and the command that reproduces them are in
[`agentcc-gateway/README.md`](../../agentcc-gateway/README.md#-benchmarks).

### On FutureAGI's hosted gateway

The hosted gateway's config for this release sets H1 to H4. H5 and H6 come from this release's
code. Together they describe the hosted gateway once it runs this release
with that config.

- **H1. Managed models and managed tools are off.** The hosted config for
  this release marks no provider `managed: true` and has no `tools`
  section, and the console offers no managed models (N7).
- **H2. The response cache is off.** The organization-scoped cache (C15)
  ships in the code, but the hosted config for this release sets
  `cache.enabled: false`. That turns the gateway's response cache off for
  every organization, including one whose own config turns it on. The
  console's Org Config > Cache tab says so ("Not enforced on this
  gateway") and keeps its two settings, Enabled and Default TTL, read-only
  while the gateway reports response caching off.
- **H3. A2A is off.** The hosted config for this release sets
  `a2a.enabled: false`, so `/a2a` answers 404.
- **H4. MCP is off.** The hosted config for this release sets
  `mcp.enabled: false`, so `/mcp` answers 404, and the MCP admin API reports
  `gateway_mcp_disabled` (C21).
- **H5. Async requests from console keys are refused.** Every API key the
  console creates is an organization key, and this release answers a chat
  completion sent with `x-agentcc-async: true` from an organization key
  with 400 `deferred_run_unavailable` (C16). Send it synchronously.
- **H6. The console hides what is not finished** (N7).
- **H7. Checks on the request read every API, and refuse what they cannot
  read** (C35). An organization whose checks on the request include one in
  block mode, or one that masks or redacts, gets 403 `request_unavailable`
  on every transcription, translation and OCR request and on embeddings
  sent as token ids, before the provider is called. Set those checks to
  warn or log to serve such requests unread.
- **H8. Checks on the request read an agent's tool calls and tool
  results** (C36, C37). An organization's PII, secrets, keyword blocklist,
  content moderation, topic and input-validation checks in block mode
  refuse a request whose tool calls' arguments hold what they block, and
  its prompt-injection check in block mode refuses an injection in a tool's
  result. A check that masks or redacts rewrites the arguments too, and a
  check that rewrites the request and does not finish within its timeout
  refuses the request (C39). Such checks also refuse, with 403
  `request_unavailable`, a request whose tool calls' arguments are nested
  more than 64 levels deep, or whose body holds a value they read nested
  more than 64 levels deep, which they cannot read (C36). The response cache stays off (H2), so C38
  changes nothing there.

### Before you upgrade a self-hosted gateway

**U1. Admin tokens must be strong, or the gateway will not start.** The
gateway exits at start-up when either token below is empty, shorter than 32
characters, has fewer than 8 distinct characters, contains a value published
in this repository (such as `agentcc-admin-secret` or the compose default
`local-dev-only-admin-token-replace-me`), or is a placeholder:

- `admin.token` (`AGENTCC_ADMIN_TOKEN`), whenever admin auth is configured;
- `control_plane.admin_token` (`AGENTCC_CONTROL_PLANE_TOKEN`), whenever a
  control-plane URL is set.

The error names the setting, never the value. Django's gateway admin
endpoints refuse the same tokens with 403. Generate one value with
`openssl rand -hex 32` and set it as both variables, on the gateway and on
Django. Setting only `AGENTCC_ADMIN_TOKEN` is not enough when a config file
holds a literal `control_plane.admin_token`. For local development only,
`AGENTCC_ALLOW_INSECURE_ADMIN_TOKEN=1` (read from the environment, never
from a config file) lets the gateway start anyway.

**U2. MCP needs a state key.** With `mcp.enabled: true`, the gateway refuses
to start until `AGENTCC_MCP_STATE_KEYS` (or `mcp.state_keys`) is set:
comma-separated keys, the current key first, each at least 32 bytes. Use one
value from `openssl rand -hex 32` per environment. To rotate, put the new
key first and keep the old one second.

**U3. IP rules read the TCP peer unless you name your proxies.** Every IP
rule (`ip_acl`, an org's `ip_acl`, a key's `allowed_ips`) checks the TCP peer
address, and reads `X-Forwarded-For` and `X-Real-IP` only from the proxies
that `server.trusted_proxies` (or `server.trusted_hops`) names. Behind a load
balancer or ingress, list the balancer's addresses in `server.trusted_proxies`
before you rely on an IP rule. The `server` section of
`agentcc-gateway/config.example.yaml` explains both settings. The gateway logs
a warning at start-up when IP rules are on and neither is set.
`ip_acl.trusted_proxies` is not a setting the gateway reads; use
`server.trusted_proxies`.

**U4. Scheduled routing stops start-up.** `routing.scheduled.enabled: true`
is refused: "routing.scheduled.enabled must be false: this gateway does not
run scheduled jobs".

**U5. FutureAGI guardrail rules need their own credentials.** A
`provider: futureagi` rule in the gateway's config file with no `api_key`,
no `secret_key`, or no `eval_id` / `eval_ids` stops start-up. An unset
`${VAR}` counts as missing. Such a rule takes its credentials from its own
settings only, never from the `FI_API_KEY` and `FI_SECRET_KEY` environment
variables. Org-config checks are covered by C3.

**U6. Plan the database migrations.** This release adds `prism` (the
`agentcc` app) migrations 0029 to 0045 and `usage` 0030. `prism` 0046 gives
the three new `agentcc_org_config` columns (`managed_models`, `tools`,
`identity`) a database default of `{}`, so a Django pod still running the
old code can save an org config during the deploy. 0030 adds 37 columns to
`agentcc_request_log` (36 nullable, and `attempt_count` with a database
default of 1) in one transaction, and it holds that table's lock while it
runs. 0031 and 0041 build their indexes concurrently. Nobody has measured
the lock time on a large request log, so run the migrations in a quiet
window. `usage` 0030 creates the managed-credit ledger tables, which stay
empty while managed models are off.

**U7. The response cache starts cold once.** The cache is empty after the
upgrade and fills again as requests are answered.

**U8. Agent-signal keys in Redis are prefixed and shorter-lived.** With
Redis configured, the outcome and affinity keys (`aout:*`, `aff:*`) carry
the gateway's Redis prefix: `redis.prefix`, else `agentcc:`. The per-request
outcome record is kept for one hour, and only for agent-tagged traffic or a
request a managed attempt served. Keys written under the old names are not
read after the upgrade, and they expire on their own. For a request that was
neither agent-tagged nor served by a managed attempt, or one older than an
hour, `POST /v1/feedback` answers 404 `generation_not_found`.

**U9. A default request timeout above the request ceiling stops start-up.**
This release adds `server.max_request_timeout`
(`AGENTCC_MAX_REQUEST_TIMEOUT`), the longest a request may run (C23). Unset,
it is `server.write_timeout`; a value above `write_timeout` is kept, and the
gateway moves each response's write deadline to that request's own deadline,
so a request that runs past `write_timeout` still gets its answer, a timeout
included. The gateway refuses to start when
`server.default_request_timeout` (`AGENTCC_DEFAULT_REQUEST_TIMEOUT`) is
above that ceiling, and the error names both values, for example
"server.default_request_timeout (10m0s) is above the ceiling
server.max_request_timeout (5m0s)". `config.example.yaml` sets
`write_timeout` and `max_request_timeout` to 300 s and
`default_request_timeout` to 60 s. Before you upgrade, check
`default_request_timeout` against the other two in the config and the
environment you deploy.

**U10. Organization guardrail settings that hold `${VAR}` make no call.** An
organization's guardrail config never reads the gateway's environment (F5): a
`futureagi` check whose `api_key`, `secret_key` or `base_url` holds `${`, or
an external-provider check with `${` in any top-level setting, makes no call,
and with action `block` every request of that organization gets 403
`content_blocked`. Replace each `${VAR}` in organization configs and
guardrail policies with the value itself. The console shows a stored
credential as `__encrypted__`: enter the value itself for every credential
of a `futureagi` or external-provider check whose value is a `${VAR}`. The
console refuses such a value with a 400 that names the field. It
also refuses to switch on such a check, to change anything else about it
while it keeps the value (its endpoint, provider or action, say), or to
activate a version that has one switched on: through the Guardrails page's
switch and edit, update-config and version activation, whatever the stored
shape.

Switching such a check off is never refused for the value it holds. Where
you switch it off depends on how the organization's guardrails were last
written:

- **By the Guardrails page's rules editor, update-config or
  update-guardrail** (stored as a `rules` list): the on/off switch
  (toggle-guardrail) switches it off.
- **By an older console save** (stored as a `checks` map): the on/off
  switch (toggle-guardrail), update-guardrail with `enabled: false` and the
  rules editor's save (update-config) each switch it off, and the section
  stays a checks map (F4).
- **By a guardrail policy** (stored as a `checks` list: every policy
  create, update, delete, apply or sync rewrites the section from the
  organization's active global policies): the on/off switch
  (toggle-guardrail), update-guardrail with `enabled: false` and
  `PATCH /agentcc/guardrail-policies/{id}/` with that check's
  `enabled: false` each switch it off in the policy it came from, so it stays
  off through later syncs (F15). Deactivating or deleting the policy also
  works, the last policy that holds a check included: the gateway then gets
  an empty guardrails section (F14).

A rules-editor save of a section a guardrail policy wrote is replaced at the
next policy sync: switch a check off there with the on/off switch, which keeps
it off (F15). A check whose stored credential holds `${` makes no call at the
gateway (F5); with action `block` the organization's requests get 403 until
the credential is entered again. `${VAR}` in the gateway's own `config.yaml`
is expanded (U5).

**U11. Run the request-log credential scrub.**
Once this release runs, run
`python manage.py agentcc_scrub_request_log_credentials` on the platform. It
reads every stored request-log row, soft-deleted ones too, and reports by
organization and key id, never by value, each gateway key and credential
header a row holds. Then run it again with
`--apply` to mask those headers in the stored rows. `--org <id>` limits it to
one organization. A hosted Django runs management commands only in its
operator bootstrap job; this command is on that job's list.

**U12. On a self-hosted gateway, list any private guardrail or alert
endpoint before you upgrade.** The gateway dials an organization's guardrail
webhooks, external guardrail services and alert channels through its
guarded egress path (F10, F11). If an organization relies on a guardrail or
alert endpoint on a private, LAN or loopback address — a self-hosted
guardrail service, an in-cluster collector — add that address to
`mcp.egress.private_allow` (a CIDR, or a `host[:port]`) before you upgrade.
Until it is listed, those checks return a call failure (a blocking check then
refuses the request) and those alerts are not delivered. A guardrail or alert
endpoint on a public address needs nothing. FutureAGI's hosted gateway sets no
`mcp.egress.private_allow`: an org check or alert channel that names a
private, LAN or loopback address is refused there. Cloud-metadata addresses
are never reachable, listed or not.

The FutureAGI eval check (`futureagi-eval`, `provider: futureagi`) is covered
too. The platform fills an org check's `base_url` with its own `BASE_URL`
(`http://localhost:8000` unless you set it). A check whose `base_url` is
unset, or is `https://api.futureagi.com` (the platform's default `BASE_URL`
on FutureAGI's cloud), goes to the gateway's `FI_BASE_URL` as before, with
nothing to list. When your `BASE_URL` is a private, LAN or loopback address,
do one of these before you upgrade: set the gateway's `FI_BASE_URL` to exactly
the same URL (the gateway trusts its own `FI_BASE_URL` and nothing else on
that host; a check naming `https://api.futureagi.com` is then sent there too,
as it already is wherever `FI_BASE_URL` is set), or list its `host:port` in
`mcp.egress.private_allow` (which opens that address to every org callback,
not only this check). Until then the check returns a call failure. The
gateway also adds the comma-separated entries of
`AGENTCC_MCP_EGRESS_PRIVATE_ALLOW` in its environment to that list, checked
as the config file's are. The Standalone install (`docker-compose.yml`) sets
it to `localhost:8000,127.0.0.1:8000` for its gateway, the API in the same
container and only its port, so there an org FutureAGI eval check whose
`base_url` is the default `BASE_URL` keeps reaching the API with guardrails
on, whatever config file is mounted.

**U13. Run the org-config secret migrations.**
Migrations `prism` 0047, 0050 (the same secrets under the gateway contract's
other spellings, such as `secretAccessKey`) and 0051 (alert channel URLs)
store each org's cache-backend credentials (Redis password, S3 secret access
key, Azure connection string and SAS token, and the semantic cache's
vector-store API keys), A2A agent tokens, audit-sink and alert-channel webhook
headers, alert channel URLs that carry a credential (a Slack incoming webhook,
a token in the path or query) and MCP server auth encrypted, in every version
of every org (F12, F22). Run all three before the new release serves traffic.
Each of these fields is write-only in the console: a value entered there
replaces the stored one, which stays until you do. Each migration
is idempotent and reversible, and commits in batches of 500 rows, each in its
own transaction: if it stops part way, the batches done stay encrypted, and
running `migrate` again finishes the rest.

**U14. A rule that rewrites the request refuses it when it does not finish,
and only logs in async mode.** A rule in the config file, or an
organization's check, that rewrites the request (`pii-detection` with
`remediation` `mask`, `redact` or `hash`, or the action `mask`) and times
out or fails refuses the request with 403 `remediation_unavailable`,
with `guardrails.fail_open` on (the default) or off (C39). Raise the rule's
`timeout` (or `guardrails.default_timeout`) if requests are refused this
way. Such a rule in `mode: async` never rewrites the request: it only logs
what it finds.

**U15. Model-hub keys are encrypted by migration `model_hub` 0125 when
`INTEGRATION_ENCRYPTION_KEY` is set; check the result after the deploy.**
Migration `model_hub` 0124 changes the two key columns to `text` (on
PostgreSQL a catalog change, no table rewrite), and 0125 encrypts every stored
provider key, custom-model credential and dataset secret with
`INTEGRATION_ENCRYPTION_KEY`, soft-deleted rows included (F21); each key reads
back unchanged. It commits in batches of 500 rows, each in its own
transaction, and a re-run finishes what is left.

- **The key must be set and kept.** FutureAGI Cloud sets
  `INTEGRATION_ENCRYPTION_KEY`, the Helm chart generates and keeps it, and the
  installer fills it; `deploy/setup.sh` does not generate it. Keep it with the
  database backups: a new value cannot read keys stored under the old one.
  Without a valid key (unset, a placeholder such as `your-fernet-key`, or a
  key of the wrong length) 0125 says how many rows it did not encrypt and what
  is wrong with the key (never its value), and the server names a malformed
  key in a warning when each process starts and on every save of a key. Set a
  valid key, restart, and run
  `python manage.py model_hub_keys_at_rest --encrypt`.
- **After the deploy, check.** `python manage.py model_hub_keys_at_rest --check`
  counts rows still in the old encoding and rows holding no key this
  deployment can read, never a value, names `INTEGRATION_ENCRYPTION_KEY` when
  it is not a valid Fernet key, and fails while any row is not encrypted;
  `--encrypt` encrypts the rest. Read both counts: a process running with a
  different or malformed key reports every encrypted row as one it cannot
  decrypt. A hosted Django runs management
  commands only in its operator bootstrap job; this command is on that job's
  list.
- **During the rollout.** The Helm chart migrates in a pre-upgrade hook,
  before the new pods start, and pods of the earlier release cannot read an
  encrypted key. A key one of those pods re-saves is counted by `--check` and
  named by row id by `--encrypt`; its organization enters it again. Keep that
  window short, or, where you run migrations yourself, run 0125 once the new
  release serves.
- **Rolling back.** First run
  `python manage.py model_hub_keys_at_rest --to-legacy --confirm`, which stores
  the keys in the old encoding again; `migrate model_hub 0124` refuses to go
  back while any key is encrypted.

**U16. An organization's budgets, tool policy and alert rules take effect on
every gateway that receives org configs, and its IP rules where the gateway
enforces them.** A gateway with
`control_plane.url` or `admin.token` set builds those four plugins whatever
its node switches (`ip_acl.enabled`, `budgets.enabled`, `tool_policy.enabled`,
`alerting.enabled`) say, so a section an org saved, and has enabled, applies
from the first request after the upgrade: an `ip_acl` allow list refuses
callers outside it where the gateway enforces it (below), a hard budget
refuses requests past it, a tool policy
strips or refuses tools, and alert rules send to the org's channels. Before
you upgrade, review the org configs you hold (Gateway > Org Config) and turn
off a section you do not want enforced. An org's `ip_acl` takes effect on a
gateway that sets `server.trusted_proxies` or `server.trusted_hops`, or whose
own `ip_acl.enabled` is on (below). An org that set none of these
sections sees no change and causes no extra Redis command beyond the
organization-level spend count every organization has (F33). The node
switches still add the gateway's own rules on top.

An org's `ip_acl` applies to the model calls of chat and text
completions, `POST /v1/responses`, `/v1/messages`, `/v1beta`, embeddings,
images, audio, rerank, OCR, search and async jobs. The key-gated routes that make no model call
(the models list, files, videos, scheduled jobs, async-job and
stored-response reads, MCP, and the A2A request itself) apply it too. The
items of an admin batch (`/-/batches`) are judged by the address that
submitted the batch, resolved as for any request, by the gateway's rules
and the org's. An A2A task's model call (with `a2a.enabled`) is judged by
the address of the A2A request that started it, as the caller's chat call
is. Calls the app's backend makes with an org's key, such as the Test
Playground and console batch jobs, come from the backend's address: an org
allow list, or the gateway's own, that leaves it out refuses them. An org's
`ip_acl` is enforced on a gateway that sets `server.trusted_proxies` (or
`server.trusted_hops`), or whose own `ip_acl.enabled` is on. On any other
gateway `GET /-/config` leaves `ip_acl` out of `org_sections_enforced` and
reports `org_sections_not_enforced: {"ip_acl": "client IP unknown
(trusted_proxies not set)"}`, the console's Security tab and Settings say the
same, and a start-up warning names the fix. Set it before an organization
relies on an IP list. With `ip_acl.enabled` on, the gateway's and each org's
rules apply to the address the gateway resolves, as before (the TCP peer
without a trusted proxy, U3).

The gateway counts an org's spend whether its budgets are on or off, and
checks each budget against the spend it counts (F33).

An org's `cache` section still needs the gateway's `cache.enabled`, and
its `rate_limiting` section `rate_limiting.enabled` (on by default). With
more than one replica, keep `redis` on: budget spend and rate limits are
then shared; without it each replica counts its own. `GET /-/config` lists
what a gateway enforces in `org_sections_enforced` (N8).

**U17. `prometheus.path` and `assistants.enabled` are read.** If your config
sets `prometheus.path`, the metrics endpoint is served there instead of
`/-/metrics` (a leading `/` is added when missing; unset keeps `/-/metrics`;
it needs the admin token): point your scraper at that path or remove the
setting. If it sets `assistants.enabled: false`, every assistants, threads and
vector-stores route answers 404 `assistants_disabled` and sends nothing
upstream. The default is `true`.

**U18. Register the alert and webhook schedules, and run prism 0048.**
Email alerts and webhook retries now run on two Temporal schedules:
`agentcc-alerts-evaluator` (every minute) and `agentcc-webhook-delivery`
(every 30 s). Run `python manage.py register_temporal_schedules` after the
deploy, as for every new schedule; until it runs, request-level alert
emails and a console Retry are still sent when Temporal accepts the start,
but threshold alerts, alerts held by a cooldown and webhook retries wait.
`prism` 0048 adds four columns (the email alert's dispatch state,
`last_error` and `last_error_at`, and the webhook's sent budget keys), each
an ADD COLUMN with a constant database default, so a pod still running the
old code can save an alert or a webhook during the deploy.

**U19. A Redis response cache uses its `tls`, `mode` and `addresses`.** The
gateway reads `cache.redis.tls`, `mode` and every entry of `addresses` (F39):

- `tls: true` makes every connection TLS 1.2 or later, verified against the
  system's CA roots for the host of the address dialled. A Redis that does
  not speak TLS, or whose certificate does not verify, stops the gateway at
  start-up; nothing is sent in plaintext.
- `mode: cluster` uses Redis Cluster, found from `addresses` (and
  `address`). `db` must be 0: a cluster has no other database.
- `mode: sentinel` reads `address` as the master set's name (the name in the
  sentinels' `sentinel monitor <name> ...`) and `addresses` as the
  sentinels. The password goes to the master only; the sentinels are asked
  without one. A `host:port` in `address` is refused with an error that says
  so.
- `mode` unset or `single`: the node at `address`, else the first of
  `addresses`, as before (a warning says how many addresses it ignores).
- Any other `mode` stops the gateway at start-up.

Check `cache.redis` before you upgrade: `tls: true` for a Redis without TLS,
or `mode: cluster` or `sentinel` for a single node, stops the gateway at
start-up with an error naming the cause.

**U20. Register the batch schedule, and run the batch-jobs migration.**
Console batch jobs are now reported and their keys revoked by a Temporal
schedule, `agentcc-batches` (every minute). Run
`python manage.py register_temporal_schedules` after the deploy, as for
every new schedule; until it runs, a batch whose page is open in the console
is still reported when it ends, and every other one waits. Migration `prism`
0048 (`0048_batch_jobs`) adds the `agentcc_batch_job` table; it only
creates a table. A batch submitted from the console before the upgrade
answers 404.

**U21. Deploy the gateway before the platform, and roll them back together.**
The platform now pushes an alert rule's `severity` (C68) to the gateway, and
a rule saved earlier from Monitoring > Create Rule already holds one. A
gateway of an older release decodes an org config strictly and answers
such a push 400 `invalid_json`, so that organization's whole config (its
providers, guardrails and budgets included) stops syncing until the gateway
is upgraded. Upgrade the gateway first, then the platform; never roll the
gateway back alone past this release.

**U22. The gateway names the config keys it does not read.** When it loads
its config file, a key that no setting reads (a misspelt key, or one an
earlier release read) is named in one warning, `config: keys the gateway
does not read were ignored`, and the gateway starts as before. Such a key
has no effect, so check the log after upgrading. The warning carries key
names only: a `${VAR}` reference is shown as `$VAR`, never its value, and a
value that a missing space joined to its key (`{api_key:...}` in a flow
mapping) is left out.

### Changes callers can see

**C1. A key's `allowed_providers` is enforced on every endpoint.** It applies
before the provider is called, on every endpoint that routes to a provider:
chat, completions, messages and count tokens, Gemini `generateContent`,
embeddings, images, the audio routes, rerank, responses, search, OCR, the
assistants and vector-store routes, and realtime. An organization's API key
that may not use the resolved provider gets 403 `permission_denied`, "API key
does not have access to provider: \<id\>". On realtime,
C43 refuses every key but an internal one before a provider is resolved, so
such a key gets C43's 403 `permission_denied` there instead. If you set
`allowed_providers` on a key, check that it names every provider the key
really uses.

**C2. `/mcp` and `/a2a` need a key, and only canonical paths are served.**
With request auth on, every method and subpath under the MCP endpoint and
`/a2a` needs a valid API key, as `/v1` does. An invalid key on `/mcp` is
a 401. A route spelled non-canonically (`//v1/...`, a trailing `/`, dot
segments, an encoded `/`) is refused: 401 without a key, and 404
`non_canonical_path`, naming the canonical path, with one. Every 401 carries
`WWW-Authenticate: Bearer realm="agentcc-gateway"`.

**C3. An incomplete FutureAGI evaluation check blocks.** An org-config
`futureagi-eval` check with no API key, no secret key or no eval id, with
action `block`, refuses the request with 403 `content_blocked`, and the
message names the check and the missing field. No call is made to the
evaluation API. On this platform, Django fills in the org's own keys when the
check's `base_url` is the platform's, so the eval id is usually what is
missing.

**C4. Video, scheduled, search and realtime routes answer 501.** With no
video provider configured (no provider adapter that ships with this release
is one), every `/v1/videos` route answers 501 `endpoint_not_configured`. So
does every `/v1/scheduled` route. No shipped adapter serves search or
realtime either: `POST /v1/search` answers 501 `not_supported`, and
`GET /v1/realtime` answers 501 to an internal key (any other key gets 403
first, C43). The gateway README says so wherever it names these routes.

**C5. Org-config saves refuse routing keys the gateway does not run.** A new
or changed `routing.scheduled`, `routing.access_groups` or `routing.mirror`
value gets 400 `unsupported_routing_key`, with `param` naming the key. A
value the active config version already holds is saved unchanged, with a
warning. Removing or clearing one is never refused. `routing.conditional_routes`
and `routing.default_model` are stored with a "stored, not enforced" warning.
Default model and conditional routes are not available on this release: the
console does not offer them (the Fallbacks page, Org Config > Routing), and a
stored value is kept as it is. Every write path applies these rules,
update-config included, and the response carries the warnings.

**C6. Reserved names in key metadata and request metadata.**

- Creating or updating an API key with any of these metadata names gets 400:
  `org_id` (other than the key's own org), `access_groups`, `type`,
  `key_type`, `role`, `allow_policy_override`, `team`, `id`, `name` and
  `owner`. `log_content` is set through its own field, not through
  `metadata`. `rate_limit_rpm`, `rate_limit_tpm` and `allowed_ips` are
  refused either way (C22). `type`, `name` and `team` are plausible labels of
  your own: rename them.
  At each key sync, the gateway drops any reserved entry a key holds.
- The gateway admin route `PUT /-/keys/{id}` answers 409
  `reserved_metadata` to a change to a reserved entry. `POST /-/keys` and
  the key sync: C41.
- Request metadata, in the `x-agentcc-metadata` header or the body's
  `metadata` field, also drops keys that start with `auto_`, `managed_`,
  `agent_`, `omega_`, `run_`, `step` or `ledger_`, and these exact keys:
  `cost_base`, `cost_markup_percent`, `billing_mode`, `usage_estimated`,
  `model_alias_requested`, `routing_options`, `upstream_provider_object`,
  `billing_alert`, `source_choice`, `outcome_class`, `ocr_pages`,
  `search_units`, `response_status`, `counted_tokens` and
  `usage_unreadable`. Keys that start with `rbac_` are reserved for the
  gateway and dropped too. The request still runs; the
  dropped key just does not reach your logs. A key named `step_count` or `run_label` is dropped too,
  so rename such dimensions. An object under `metadata.agent` or
  `metadata.omega` is read as the request's agent context, not as a
  dimension. C40 reserves more names.

**C7. Agent context headers.** Chat completions, embeddings, rerank and
capability calls read an agent context from request headers named
`x-agentcc-<field>` (each also accepted as `x-omega-<field>`) and from the
body object `metadata.agent` (or `metadata.omega`) under the field's
snake_case name (`run-id` is `run_id`). The gateway records it in the
request log and never sends it upstream. The fields and the values each
accepts:

- `agent-id`, `agent-version`, `run-id`, `parent-run-id`, `task-id`,
  `trace-id`, `workflow`, `attempt-id`, `budget-scope` (also `ledger-id`)
  and `workspace-id`: 1 to 128 characters of `A-Z a-z 0-9 . _ : @ / -`.
- `user-id`: 1 to 255 bytes of printable characters (letters, marks,
  numbers, punctuation, symbols and the space), so `jane+tag@example.com`,
  `user 42` and a 200-character id are accepted. Control characters (tab,
  newline, NUL, DEL), invisible characters (zero-width characters, spaces
  other than U+0020, the byte-order mark, line separators) and invalid UTF-8
  are refused. When the call sends neither `x-agentcc-user` nor a body
  `user`, it is the request's user in the request log.
- `env` and `framework`: 1 to 32 characters of `A-Z a-z 0-9 . _ : @ / -`.
- `step`: one of `plan`, `act`, `tool`, `extract`, `verify`, `reflect`,
  `memory`, `coordinate`, `summarize`, `final`, `other`.
- `turn`: an integer from 0 to 100000.
- `budget-usd`, `budget-remaining-usd`, `max-cost-usd`: a decimal number of
  US dollars from 0 to 1000000 (C50).
- `deadline-ms`: an integer number of milliseconds from 1 to 3600000 (C50).
- `route-hint`: `cost`, `balanced`, `quality` or `latency`; `require`: a
  comma-separated list of `tools`, `json_schema`, `vision`, `reasoning`,
  `audio`, `long_context`; `prev-outcome`: `ok`, `empty`, `verify_failed`
  or `retried`.

The session id is `x-agentcc-session-id` (also `x-omega-session-id`): any
value of at most 255 bytes. A longer `x-agentcc-session-id` gets 400
`session_id_too_long`, a longer `x-omega-session-id` 400
`agent_header_invalid`. `x-session-id`, the body's `session_id` and
`metadata.session_id` are read only when neither header is sent, and only
when they are at most 255 bytes.

Two errors refuse an agent context, both 400 with `error.param` naming the
header or body key:

- `agent_header_invalid`: a value its field does not accept;
  `error.message` states the field's rule (for example
  `x-agentcc-user-id must be 1 to 255 bytes of printable characters: ...`).
  A body field of the wrong JSON type and an `x-agentcc-user` longer than
  256 characters get it too.
- `agent_header_conflict`: the same field sent twice with different values
  (a header and its `x-omega-` alias, or a header and the body).

By default the gateway's CORS list lets a browser send three agent
headers, `x-agentcc-user-id`, `x-agentcc-session-id` and
`x-agentcc-trace-id`, and none of the others. A
`cors.allowed_headers` list replaces the default one, so an operator who
allows more headers lists every header browsers send.

**C8. Embeddings.**

- `input_type` must be `query`, `document`, `classification` or
  `clustering` (400 `invalid_input_type`).
- On a provider with no such parameter (OpenAI, Azure and Bedrock embeddings),
  any `input_type` gets 400 `input_type_unsupported`. Cohere and Gemini take
  it.
- An embedding request makes exactly one upstream attempt, with no failover
  to another host and no model fallback, because vectors from another model
  or host live in another vector space. A failed target returns its own
  error. The response header `x-agentcc-embedding-space` names the vector
  space.
- When Gemini reports no usage, the gateway logs an estimate marked
  `usage_estimated`.
- Cohere rerank is billed by its `search_units`.

**C9. Image models past their shutdown date answer 410.** From a model's
shutdown date, a request for it gets 410 `model_expired`, naming the
replacement. The calendar is `agentcc-gateway/catalog/deprecations.json`.
Nine entries were already past on 2026-10-18: `dall-e-2` and `dall-e-3`
(replacement `gpt-image-2`); `gemini-2.5-flash-image` and
`imagen-3.0-generate-002` (replacement `gemini-3.1-flash-image`); and, on the
Gemini API, `imagen-4.0-generate-001`, `imagen-4.0-fast-generate-001`,
`imagen-4.0-ultra-generate-001` and `gemini-3.1-flash-image-preview`
(replacement `gemini-3.1-flash-image`) and `gemini-3-pro-image-preview`
(replacement `gemini-3-pro-image`). Next: `gpt-image-1` on 2026-10-23;
`gpt-image-1-mini`, `gpt-image-1.5`, `gpt-image-1.5-2025-12-16` and
`chatgpt-image-latest` on 2026-12-01. Azure keeps some of these longer, and
its own dates are in the file. There is no switch to turn this off.

**C10. A non-chat request never takes a model fallback.** A non-chat request
(embeddings, rerank, images, audio and the rest) for a model that no
provider serves gets that model's own error, even when the config names a
fallback model for it. A route that overrides the model serves a non-chat
request with the route's model. On `/v1/images/generations`, `/v1/ocr`,
`/v1/audio/speech`, `/v1/audio/translations` and `/v1/audio/transcriptions`
the gateway also reports that model (`x-agentcc-model-used`, and
`resolved_model` in the request log) and prices the request on it: the cost
estimate, which budgets count, is that model's. If the gateway has no price
for the route's model (a deployment name, say), it prices the request on the
requested model instead, as it does on chat, and `cost_source` names where
that price came from (`modeldb`, for example) rather than `unpriced`. Either
way, a per-model budget counts the request under the requested model.

**C11. Native server tools are costed and checked.**

- On BYOK `/v1/messages` and Gemini `generateContent` calls, the cost
  estimate now includes server-tool fees: Anthropic web search and web fetch
  counts, and Gemini grounding. Such an attempt is marked `usage_estimated`,
  because the fee is the gateway's estimate of your own provider's bill.
- A request with a server tool, pinned to a provider that cannot run it,
  gets 403 `model_not_permitted` with `metadata.reason`
  `server_tool_unsupported`, and no upstream is called.
- Gemini grounded responses are never stored in the response cache, because
  the Gemini API terms forbid it.

**C12. The circuit breaker is per model.** One failing model opens its own
circuits, not its provider's, so the provider keeps serving its other
models. A request whose every target has an open circuit gets 503
`all_providers_unavailable` without reaching an upstream.

**C13. Anthropic-dialect usage on `/v1/messages`.** When `/v1/messages` is
served by a provider that is not Anthropic's own API (Bedrock Claude, for
example), the usage follows Anthropic's format, streaming or not:
`input_tokens` excludes cached tokens, and `cache_read_input_tokens` and
`cache_creation_input_tokens` are reported on their own.

**C14. Cache hits do not return the original charge.** A response served from
the cache carries no `usage.cost_details`, `usage.is_byok` or stored upstream
`provider`. Token counts are as stored.

**C15. Stored objects, A2A tasks and MCP sessions belong to their org.**

- Files, stored responses, async jobs, video jobs and A2A tasks are scoped
  to the organization of the key that created them. Another organization's
  id gets the same answer as an id that does not exist (404; for A2A, the
  JSON-RPC error `task not found` at HTTP 200), and the object is left
  untouched. Scheduled jobs cannot be created in this build (C4, U4).
- Keys with no organization, and every request when request auth is off, form
  one no-org tenant, apart from every organization: neither sees the other's
  objects.
- An MCP session belongs to the key that opened it. Another key, even one in
  the same organization, gets 404 for its session id. After you rotate a
  key, the client must initialize a new session (MCP clients do this on a
  404).
- Response-cache entries are never shared between organizations.

**C16. Async requests from organization API keys are refused.** A chat
completion (`/v1/chat/completions`, the only route that reads the header)
with `x-agentcc-async: true`, sent with an organization's API key, gets 400
`deferred_run_unavailable`: "async inference is not available for
organization API keys; send the request synchronously". This covers
every key that is not an internal service key, managed requests included.
No job is stored and no provider is called.

Internal service keys, and every request on a gateway with request auth
off, can still queue async jobs, with these changes:

- Jobs run on the routing the request resolved when it was sent.
- One organization runs at most `async.max_workers_per_org` jobs at once
  (by default half of `async.max_workers`, so 5 of 10) and queues at most
  half of the queue's 1,000 slots. Every request on a gateway with auth off
  counts as one tenant, so such a gateway runs at most 5 jobs at once unless
  you raise the cap.
- A job's run time is capped at `async.max_job_timeout`. By default this is
  the longest timeout in the config.
- A job that cannot be queued is refused at once, with 429
  `async_org_queue_full` or 503 `async_queue_full`, and is not stored.
- `DELETE /v1/async/{id}` cancels the running provider call.
- A request authorized by a license token gets 400 `async_not_supported`.

**C17. IP rules are checked on every route a key opens.** The key gate applies
a key's `allowed_ips` and the gateway's own `ip_acl` on every route it
gates, files, video, responses, async and scheduled jobs, MCP and A2A
included, and an organization's `ip_acl` there on a gateway that enforces
it (U16, N8). It checks the client address described in U3, so a rule means what
that address means: behind a proxy, set `server.trusted_proxies` first. A
key's `allowed_ips` applies only to keys in the gateway's config file: the
platform's API does not offer it (C22).

**C18. The guardrail policy header needs permission.** When the gateway has
any guardrail rule in its config file, `X-Guardrail-Policy` from a key
without `allow_policy_override` gets 403 before the provider is called, and
an invalid value gets 400. Every route a check on the request reads (C32)
reads the header, as `/v1/chat/completions` and `/v1/completions` do:
`/v1/messages` and its `count_tokens`, `/v1beta` `generateContent`,
`streamGenerateContent` and `countTokens`, and `/v1/responses`, streamed, not
streamed or in the background, whether the request is passed through to the
provider's own API or translated. A key with `allow_policy_override` gets
`log-only`, `disabled` and `strict` there as on chat, and a key without it
that sends the header gets 403 before the provider is called. The routes in
C35 read it too, the same way: `/v1/embeddings`, `/v1beta` `embedContent`,
images, the audio routes, rerank, search, OCR, `/v1/videos`, the Assistants
API and vector store search.

**C19. Deleting a key revokes it on the gateway.** Deleting a key in the
console revokes it on the gateway too. If the gateway cannot revoke it, the
delete answers 503, and the key is left revoked and listed. A revocation
reaches every replica that shares the gateway's Redis at once, and the others
at their next key sync. A key created on that replica stays there until a key
sync that starts at least `control_plane.key_sync_grace` (30 seconds by
default) after the key was created. A key the gateway has revoked stays
revoked even when a key sync that read the key list just before the revocation
lands just after it.

**C20. A user removed from every organization sees nothing.** The shared list
views return nothing for a request with no organization: no gateway keys,
request logs or configs.

**C21. The MCP admin API tells "MCP is off" apart from a failing gateway.**
On the platform's gateway admin API:

- `GET /agentcc/gateways/{id}/mcp-status/` reports
  `reason: gateway_mcp_disabled` for every organization when the gateway runs
  with MCP off, including organizations with no MCP server.
- `mcp-tools/`, `mcp-resources/` and `mcp-prompts/` answer 404 with code
  `gateway_mcp_disabled` when MCP is off, and 502 with code
  `gateway_unreachable` when the gateway fails or cannot be reached.
- `test-mcp-tool/` answers 404 `gateway_mcp_disabled` when MCP is off.
- An unreachable gateway still reads as `mcp-status` 200 with
  `reason: gateway_unreachable`.
- On a gateway whose MCP status is not org-scoped (no `org_enabled`: its one
  MCP registry comes from its own config file, never from an organization's
  servers), `mcp-status` reports `reason: gateway_not_org_scoped` for every
  organization, `mcp-tools/`, `mcp-resources/` and `mcp-prompts/` answer an
  empty list, and `test-mcp-tool/` answers 400 as for an organization with no
  MCP server, without running the tool.

**C22. API keys made through the platform take no `rate_limit_rpm`,
`rate_limit_tpm` or `allowed_ips`.** Creating or updating a key with any of
them, as a field or as a `metadata` name and with any value (`null` included),
fails with 400 `key_limit_not_enforced`, which names the fields, and nothing
is saved. Reads do not show the three names in a key's `metadata`. The
organization's `per_key_rpm` and `per_key_tpm` (C66) hold every key of the
organization.

**C23. One timeout ceiling holds every request.**
`server.max_request_timeout` (unset: `server.write_timeout`, 300 s by
default; U9) is the longest a request may run, whatever its timeout header
or model timeout asks for.

- Chat completions is held at it.
- A route that resolves no timeout of its own (the gateway's admin routes,
  `/v1/models`, files and the like) ends at the ceiling.
- On a route that resolves its own timeout (embeddings, rerank, images, audio
  and the rest), a model timeout above `default_request_timeout` holds, up to
  the ceiling.
- A timeout header (`x-agentcc-timeout` or `x-agentcc-request-timeout`) sets a
  request's timeout up to the ceiling: a longer value is held at it.
- Three things are not held at it. The MCP endpoint and its OAuth metadata
  have no request deadline, so the MCP event stream stays open. An async
  job (C16) runs to the timeout its request resolved before the ceiling,
  and `async.max_job_timeout` caps it. A relayed speech session
  (`GET /deepgram/...`, `/cartesia/...`) keeps relaying past the ceiling once its
  WebSocket is open, until either side closes it or the relay's own idle and
  session limits end it.

**C24. `x-agentcc-timeout-ms` reports the limit that applied.** The response
header carries the shorter of the timeout the gateway resolved for the request
and the client timeout of the provider instance that served it, when that
provider states one. On chat it reads the instance that served the response,
after any retry or failover.

**C25. A blocked chat answer gets 403 `content_blocked`, and its log entry
says so.** When a guardrail on the output blocks the answer to a
`/v1/chat/completions` request, the request gets 403 `content_blocked` (type
`guardrail_error`), and the message names the check, for a rule in the
gateway's config file and an organization's output check in block mode alike.
Of an organization's checks, only `data-leakage-prevention` and
`hallucination-detection` read the answer; the others read the request only.
An answer from a cache hit that the output check blocks gets the same 403, for
a streaming request too. The request log records status 403, the error and
`guardrail_action: blocked`. The other routes are in C26. On a
stream:

- A streamed answer that a blocking check reads is held until the check
  passes, so it starts when the answer is complete. A blocking check is a
  rule in the config file at the post stage whose action is `block`, an
  organization's check on the answer in block mode, or the streaming
  checker with `failure_action: stop`. The response's status and headers
  go out at once; its body waits. The checks read the answer the gateway
  assembles for them (C30); tool-call frames in a held stream are not read
  by the checks. A passing answer then goes out chunk for chunk as the
  provider sent it, with its usage as C79 says.
- A blocked one carries none of the answer and ends with the guardrail's
  error event: `content_blocked`, or `stream_blocked` from the streaming
  checker, then the usage chunk when the request asked for usage, then
  `[DONE]`. When the streaming checker stops the stream before the
  provider's stream ends, its error event is the whole stream. The log
  entry records 403 and `guardrail_action: blocked`, and the response
  cache never stores the blocked answer.
- A held stream that ends before the provider's stream does (the
  request's deadline, a client gone, or a provider error mid-stream) is
  checked on the part it read, and goes out or is refused the same way.
  The streaming checker's final check reads the text after its last
  check. Every check runs to its end, within its own timeout, even when
  the request's deadline fires while a check is running; only a check's
  own timeout or failure counts for `guardrails.fail_open`.
- The streaming checker runs the config file's rules under the policy the
  other checks use: the organization's, with the key's overrides on top.
  An organization's override that makes a rule block holds and blocks a
  chat stream, and one that makes a blocking rule warn holds nothing.
- A held stream buffers at most 10 MiB, the size the gateway reads of a
  provider's answer. A stream over it, or an answer the checks cannot read
  whole, is refused the same way, with `answer_unavailable` naming the
  check.
- With the streaming checker in disclaimer mode and no other blocking
  check on the answer, the stream is not held: the answer goes out as it
  comes, the disclaimer follows it, and no error event follows. A request
  with no blocking check on the answer streams as it comes.

**C26. A blocked answer is withheld on the other routes too.** What C25 says
for `/v1/chat/completions` holds on `/v1/messages`, `/v1beta`
`generateContent`, `/v1/completions` and `/v1/responses`, whether the
gateway translates the request for the provider or passes it through to the
provider's own API, and for async jobs, batch items and A2A tasks. The
request gets the guardrail's 403 in that API's own error format: Anthropic's
`permission_error`, Google's `PERMISSION_DENIED`, or `content_blocked` (type
`guardrail_error`) on the OpenAI-format routes, and the request log records
the same status. An async job fails with the guardrail's error, a batch item
is an error that names the check, and an A2A task fails with the guardrail's
error (`Pipeline error: content_blocked: …`), never as a provider that sent
no answer. A stream on these routes that a blocking check reads is held as
C25 says, translated or passed through: a blocked one carries none of the
answer, not in a delta, a done event or a completed event, and ends with the
guardrail's error event in the route's own format: on `/v1/completions` the
error event, then the usage chunk when the request asked for usage, then
`[DONE]`; on `/v1/messages` an `error` event; on `streamGenerateContent` a
data frame that holds the error; and on `/v1/responses` an `error` event.
The checks read the answer on all of these routes (C30); where the gateway
cannot, a check that would block refuses the request (C31).

**C27. A `/v1/completions` stream request keeps a refusal's status.** A
streaming `/v1/completions` request refused before its stream starts, for
example by a guardrail on the request, gets the refusal's own status and
error (403 `content_blocked` for a guardrail), as on every other route.

**C28. A guardrail's refusal on a translated `/v1/messages` or `/v1beta`
request is a permission error.** When the gateway translates such a request
for a provider of another format, a guardrail's 403 carries Anthropic's
`permission_error` or Google's `PERMISSION_DENIED`, as the pass-through routes
do.

**C29. A translated `streamGenerateContent` stream ends when the provider's
does.** A `/v1beta` `streamGenerateContent` request served by a provider of
another format ends with the provider's stream, and its cost and log entry are
recorded then.

**C30. A check on the answer reads it on every route that returns one.** A
check that reads the answer's text (an organization's
`data-leakage-prevention` or `hallucination-detection`, or a rule in the
gateway's config file at the post stage) reads the answer the client
gets on `/v1/chat/completions`, `/v1/completions`, `/v1/messages`, `/v1beta`
`generateContent` and `streamGenerateContent`, and `/v1/responses`: passed
through to the provider's own API or translated, streamed or not, whether or
not the request log keeps bodies. A block then works as C25 and C26 say. The
answer is withheld (403 in the route's format) when it is not a stream. A
streamed answer that a blocking check reads is held until the check passes,
so it starts when the answer is complete; a blocked one carries none of the
answer and ends with the guardrail's error event. The log records 403 and
`guardrail_action: blocked`. Every choice is
read: every choice of a `/v1/completions` answer (`n` above 1, or a list of
prompts), every candidate of a Gemini answer, and each choice of a stream on
its own. On `/v1/completions` to an OpenAI-compatible provider, the request
log's `response_body`, when the log keeps bodies, holds every choice of the
answer. With the streaming checker on, a chat stream
is assembled for an organization's checks only. The checker already reads
the stream for the config file's rules as it goes. Where a check reads the
answer of a request passed through to the provider's own API, the request
log's `response_body`, when the log keeps bodies, holds that answer as a chat
answer.

**C31. When no check can read the answer, a check that would block it
refuses the request.** A `/v1/responses` request with `background: true` is
answered later, and its answer never passes through the gateway. When a
check on the answer would block, the request is refused before the
provider is called, with 403 (type `guardrail_error`, code
`answer_unavailable`). The message names the check and says that a
background response's answer does not pass through the gateway. Send the
request without `background` to have it checked. A 200 answer the gateway
cannot read (not the API's JSON) on `/v1/responses`, or on `/v1/messages` or
`/v1beta` passed through to the provider's own API, is withheld with the
same error when such a check would block it. "Would block" means a rule in
the config file at the post stage whose action is `block` after the key's
policy and `X-Guardrail-Policy`, or an organization's check that reads the
answer in block mode. With such checks only in warn or log mode, or none,
these requests run as before. The request log records the refusal as a
guardrail block, with `answer_unavailable` in its metadata.

**C32. A check on the request reads it on every route that sends one to a
chat model.** A check that reads the request (an organization's PII,
prompt-injection, keyword blocklist, secrets, topic, language, system
prompt protection or other check that runs before the provider, or a rule in
the gateway's config file at the pre stage) reads the request on
`/v1/messages` sent to an Anthropic-format provider, on `/v1beta`
`generateContent` and `streamGenerateContent` sent to a Google-format
provider, and on `/v1/responses`, streamed, not streamed or in the
background. It also reads the prompt the provider gets to count its tokens,
on `/v1/messages/count_tokens` and `/v1beta` `countTokens` (as `contents`,
or as a `generateContentRequest`). `/v1/messages/count_tokens` to an
Anthropic-format provider goes through the gateway's pipeline as
`/v1/messages` and `/v1beta` `countTokens` do: rate limits,
request quotas and budgets apply to it, and it is logged, so each call is
metered as a gateway request. Counting tokens therefore counts against the
key's and the organization's rate limits, request quotas and budgets on
both count routes, as any other request does. Each count is logged with the
status the client got (the provider's own when the provider refused the
count) and with the count the provider returned, under `counted_tokens` in
the entry's metadata. The entry's token counts stay 0: counting is free at
the provider and consumes no tokens, so the count is never priced as the
request's usage. A `/v1beta` `embedContent` is logged with its status the
same way. The gateway passes these requests through to the provider's own API
as the client sent them, after the checks on the request read them. A request
a check in block mode refuses is refused before the provider is called, with
the guardrail's 403 in the route's own error format (Anthropic's
`permission_error`, Google's `PERMISSION_DENIED`, `content_blocked` of type
`guardrail_error` on `/v1/responses`); a stream is refused before it starts.
The request log records 403 and `guardrail_action: blocked`. The checks read
such a request as the gateway's translators give it to a provider of another
format: the system prompt (Anthropic's `system`, Gemini's system instruction,
the Responses `instructions`), every turn's text, tool results and tool calls,
and the tools by name. They also read a document's and a search result's
text on `/v1/messages`, Gemini function responses and code execution
results, and Responses tool outputs and a stored prompt's variables. The
output of a tool the provider runs is read too: a command's `stdout` and
`stderr` (Anthropic code execution and bash code execution results,
Responses `shell_call_output`), a text editor result's file text and changed
lines, the page a web fetch result holds, a search result's title, an
`apply_patch_call_output` and a code interpreter's logs. A Gemini function
response is read as Google decodes it before the model reads it, so text a
client wrote with JSON escapes (`\u002d`, or the escapes Python's
`json.dumps` writes for non-ASCII letters) is read as the characters it
stands for. Gemini fields are read under either name Google's API takes
(`systemInstruction` or `system_instruction`, ...), and a repeated field
given as one object (`"contents": {...}`, `"parts": {...}`, as Google's own
examples send them) is read as a list of one. A check on the tools (an
organization's Tool Permissions check) reads each tool by one name: a
function by its name, a tool the provider runs by its type (Responses
`web_search`, `file_search`, ...; Anthropic server tools by their `name`)
or, on Gemini, by its field in snake_case (`google_search`,
`code_execution`, `url_context`, whichever spelling the request uses), and
an MCP server as `mcp:<its name>` (Anthropic `mcp_servers` and
`mcp_toolset` tools, the Responses `server_label`), so `mcp:*` matches
every named MCP server. The checks at the post stage read the same request, as they read
a chat request. When the gateway cannot read such a request (it does not
have the shape its API defines), the request is refused before the provider
is called with 403 `request_unavailable` (type `guardrail_error`, or the
route's own format) if a check on the request would block it or rewrite it
(C33), and the message names the check and says why. "Would block" means a
rule in the config file at the pre stage whose action is `block` after the
key's policy and after `X-Guardrail-Policy`, which every route here reads
(C18), or an organization's check that reads
the request in block mode. "Would rewrite"
means such a rule, or an organization's check, whose remediation rewrites
the request, in any mode: its rewrite could never be applied. Otherwise the
request goes on unread, with `request_unavailable` in the log's metadata.
The same holds on native `/v1/completions` for a prompt of token ids, which
the provider decodes and the gateway cannot read.

**C33. A check's rewrite of the request reaches the provider on every
route.** `pii-detection` with remediation `mask`, `redact` or `hash`
rewrites the text it reads. On `/v1/chat/completions` the provider gets the
rewritten request. On the routes that pass the request through as the client
sent it, the gateway writes the rewrite into the body it sends:

- `/v1/messages` to an Anthropic-format provider: into the system prompt,
  text blocks, tool results (the provider's own tools' output included) and
  documents' text.
- `/v1beta` `generateContent` and `streamGenerateContent` to a
  Google-format provider: into the system instruction, text parts, function
  responses (their JSON text as Google decodes it) and code execution
  results.
- `/v1/messages/count_tokens` and `/v1beta` `countTokens`, as on the routes
  above, so the provider counts the tokens of the request it would get.
- `/v1/responses`, streamed, not streamed or in the background: into the
  instructions, input text and refusal parts, tool outputs and a stored
  prompt's variables.
- `/v1/completions` to an OpenAI-compatible provider, which gets the prompt
  as the client sent it: into the prompt, or each prompt of a list.

Each rewritten text goes back to the place it was read from, as do the
rewritten arguments of a tool call (C36). Everything else in the body
(`cache_control`, a tool call's name and id, other fields) is sent as the
client sent it, apart from white space between the fields of the objects and
lists that hold a rewritten text or rewritten arguments. When a masked match runs across two text blocks
of one turn, the two blocks are merged into the first. When the rewrite
cannot be written (a match that runs across two fields of one content block
or two prompts of a `/v1/completions` list, or a Gemini function response
the rewrite makes other than JSON, as a mask inside a number does), the
request is refused before the provider is called with 403
`remediation_unavailable`, naming the check, and the log records the block.
A request the gateway cannot read at all is refused before any check runs
(C32), as is a `/v1/completions` prompt of token ids. On the routes that
translate the request for a provider of another format, the provider gets
the rewritten request, as before.

**C34. An organization's check takes the action `mask` where it can rewrite
the request.** An organization's `pii-detection` with
the action `mask` replaces each match in the request with `***` and lets
the request through, as in log mode (no warning header), on every route in
C33. Where the gateway passes the request through as the client sent it, it
writes the mask into the body it sends, and it refuses the request before
the provider is called, as C32 and C33 say for a check that rewrites the
request, when it cannot read the body (`request_unavailable`) or cannot
write the mask into it (`remediation_unavailable`). A `remediation` of
`redact` or `hash` in the check's config rewrites that way instead: the
action turns the rewrite on, and the remediation says how. The request log
records the check with the action `mask`. The console offers `mask` only
for a check whose type can rewrite the request it reads, which is
`pii-detection` (`pii-detector` in a section stored as rules); every other
check offers block, warn and log, in the Rules tab and in the Overview's
edit dialog. On a check of any other type the gateway still reads `mask` as
`log` (see [Known limits](#known-limits)): a dialog that opens on such a
stored action shows it, disabled, as "runs as Log", and a save that changes
nothing keeps it. Which checks take which actions is in
`api_contracts/gateway/guardrail-check-actions.json`, which the gateway's
test and the console's test both read.

**C35. A check on the request reads every other API's input.** A check on the
request (an organization's check that runs before the provider, or a rule in
the gateway's config file at the pre stage) reads each of these:

- `/v1/embeddings`: the input, a string or each string of a list.
- `/v1beta` `embedContent`: the content's text parts and the title.
- `/v1/images/generations`: the prompt.
- `/v1/audio/speech` and `/v1/audio/speech/stream`: the input text.
- `/v1/audio/transcriptions` and `/v1/audio/translations`: the prompt.
- `/v1/rerank`: the query and each document.
- `/v1/search`: the query, a string or each string of a list. No provider in
  this release serves search, so the route answers 501.
- `/v1/videos`: the prompt, before the job is stored. No provider in this
  release serves video, so the route answers 501.
- The Assistants API: an assistant's instructions and tools (create and
  update), a thread's messages, a message, a run's instructions, additional
  instructions, additional messages and tools, the thread and run of
  `/v1/threads/runs`, and each tool output a run is given. A message is read
  as its text or its text parts, and an image URL is read as an image.
- `/v1/vector_stores/{id}/search`: the query, a string or each string of a
  list.

The rules of C32, C33 and C34 hold on every one of them. A request a check
in block mode refuses gets the guardrail's 403 `content_blocked` naming the
check (Google's `PERMISSION_DENIED` on `/v1beta`) before the provider is
called, and a refused video request stores no job. A request log entry of
a refused request records 403 and the block. A check's rewrite (`mask`,
`redact`, `hash`) goes into the field the provider reads, keeping the
input's shape (a string stays a string, a list keeps its other items), or
the request is refused with 403 `remediation_unavailable`. The gateway reads
the request it sends, not the body as spelled: a field written in another
case or twice is read as the provider gets it. An organization's Tool
Permissions check reads the tools an assistant or a run offers, by name (a
function by its name, `code_interpreter` and `file_search` by their type).
`X-Guardrail-Policy` applies on all of them (C18). A request log entry
records the result of each check that triggers and the action taken
(`guardrail_action`). The other requests of the Assistants, Vector Stores
and Files APIs run the pipeline C80 describes. An Assistants API body sent
empty (`POST /v1/threads` with no body creates an empty thread) holds
nothing to read, and goes on as `{}` does.

**Input the checks cannot read is refused when a check
would block or rewrite it.** Embeddings given as token ids, the document of
an OCR request (a document or image URL, inline data, or a file at the
provider) and the audio file of a transcription or translation are not text
the checks can read. When a check on the request would block the request
or rewrite it (C32's "would block" and "would rewrite"), such a request is
refused before the provider is called, with 403 `request_unavailable` (type
`guardrail_error`) naming the check and saying why, for example "Request
blocked by guardrail: pii-detection — it checks the request, and the
gateway cannot read this request: its audio file is not text the checks
can read". This holds even when a transcription's prompt passes: the prompt
is read, the audio is not. It holds for any check on the request in block
mode, whatever its type: an organization whose only such check is Tool
Permissions, which could never match audio or a document, is refused too.
An organization with a check on the request in block mode, or one that
masks or redacts the request, therefore cannot transcribe, translate or run
OCR through the gateway until it sets those checks to warn or log (without
a remediation); a key with
`allow_policy_override` can send `X-Guardrail-Policy: log-only` to run the
config file's rules as log (C18; an organization's checks ignore the
header). Without such a check the request goes on, with
`request_unavailable` in the log's metadata, and a check in warn or log mode
still reads a transcription's or translation's prompt: the response
carries `x-agentcc-guardrail-triggered: true` on a match, and the log
records it as logged or warned.

The console's Guardrails page (Overview, "Requests Before LLM checks read")
lists every route a check on the request reads, what it reads there and what
it refuses unread. The list is
`api_contracts/gateway/guardrail-request-routes.json`, which the gateway's
test and the console's test both read; the gateway's test checks that every
POST route the gateway registers under `/v1`, `/v1beta` and `/a2a`, and the
batch API, is in it, either as a route a check reads or as one that sends
no text a model reads (`/v1/files`, which the gateway keeps;
`/v1/count_tokens`, which it counts itself; the metadata-only Assistants and
vector store writes; `/v1/scheduled`, refused; `/v1/feedback`; the test
capability). This gateway has no route for `/v1/moderations`,
`/v1/images/edits` or `/v1/images/variations`: they answer 404, and no
provider gets the request.

**C36. A check on the request reads the arguments of the request's tool
calls, and a rewrite rewrites them.** The arguments are a chat request's
`tool_calls[].function.arguments`, an Anthropic `tool_use` block's `input`, a
Gemini `functionCall`'s `args`, and on `/v1/responses` a `function_call`'s or
`mcp_call`'s `arguments`, a `custom_tool_call`'s `input`, a shell call's
`action`, an `apply_patch_call`'s `operation` and a code interpreter call's
`code` (a string, or, where a client sends another JSON value in its place,
that value as JSON). `pii-detection`, `secret-detection`,
`keyword-blocklist`, `content-moderation`, `topic-restriction`,
`input-validation`'s deny and require patterns, and the external and FutureAGI
checks (in the text they send) read them, on `/v1/chat/completions` and every
route of C32. Arguments that are JSON are read value by value, up to 64 levels
of nesting, with JSON escapes decoded (`\u002d` is read as `-`). Each member
is read with its key, as `key: value`, so a pattern that needs a keyword
matches it (`secret-detection`'s password pattern matches
`{"password": "..."}`); a member whose value is an object or a list is read by
its key, and what it holds in turn. Arguments that are not JSON (a custom
tool's free text) are read whole. A tool call's name is not read.
`input-validation`'s length rules count each message's content only.
`topic-restriction` also reads text given as content parts (a tool result as
parts).

Arguments nested more than 64 levels deep (an object or a list inside 64
others) are not read: they are input the gateway cannot read, recorded as
`request_unavailable` in the request log, and a check on the request that
would block or rewrite the request refuses it before the provider is
called, 403 `request_unavailable`, as for embeddings sent as token ids
(C35); under a check that only warns or logs the request goes on.
Arguments that begin with more than 64 `[` or `{` are refused the same way,
free text included: the depth is checked before the gateway knows whether
they are JSON, and the refusal says "nested more than 64 levels deep or
begin with more than 64 [ or { brackets". The
gateway reads each tool call's arguments in one pass over their bytes,
once for all the checks on the request, before they run (and once more for
the checks on the answer that read the request); a check that outlives its
timeout stops reading.

A body is read up to 64 levels deep too. Where the gateway reads a body it
passes through (C32) or proxies as sent (C35) by walking a value on its own
(an item, content part or block of a type it does not know, the result of
a tool the provider runs, a Gemini `functionResponse`, a block inside an
Anthropic tool result, search result or document), it reads that value up
to 64 levels deep: an object or a list inside 64 others is not read. Such a
request is input the gateway cannot read in full: a check on the request
that would block or rewrite it refuses it before the provider is called,
403 `request_unavailable` naming the check and saying where (for example
"Request blocked by guardrail: pii-detection — it checks the request, and
the gateway cannot read this request: input[1] holds a value nested more
than 64 levels deep"), with `request_unavailable` in the request log; under
a check that only warns or logs the request goes on, and the checks read
the rest of it. The gateway reads a body in one pass over its
bytes (it finds where each object and list ends, then decodes each once),
writes a check's rewrite back the same way, and stops reading at its next
step once the request's deadline passes or the client goes away (checking
the body is JSON, and decoding one long string, are single steps). The prompt
a prepaid `/v1/responses` stream is charged for when its last event cannot be
read (F3) is estimated from the body read the same way, whether or not the
client is still connected.

`pii-detection` with `mask`, `redact` or `hash` rewrites each match in the
arguments where it is: in a value, or in a key that matches; a match that
starts in a key and runs into its value (a passport keyword and its number)
rewrites the value's part. Arguments that are JSON stay JSON, a rewritten
number is written as a string (with `redact`, `{"card":4111111111111111}`
becomes `{"card":"[REDACTED:credit_card]"}`), and arguments with no match
keep their bytes; a rewrite keeps every byte inside the arguments' JSON
value that it does not rewrite, white space and escapes included (white
space before and after that value is not kept). On `/v1/chat/completions`
and the translated routes the provider gets the rewritten arguments; on the
routes that pass the body through they are written back where they were
read, as JSON into a JSON value and as a string into a string, or the
request is refused with 403 `remediation_unavailable` when they cannot be
(a rewrite that is not JSON where the body holds a JSON value), as in C33.
The request log records the rewritten arguments. Two matches that overlap
(a URL whose credentials look like an email address) are rewritten as one.

**C37. prompt-injection reads a tool's result and the system prompt.**
It reads a tool's result (a `tool` or `function` message, an Anthropic
`tool_result`, a Gemini `functionResponse`, a Responses tool output) with
every pattern, as it reads a user message. It reads the system and developer
messages (Anthropic's `system`, Gemini's system instruction, the Responses
`instructions`) for the signs of text someone else wrote: a chat-template
token or a fake role tag, a JSON message that claims the system or developer
role, a jailbreak persona and a request to decode hidden text. The patterns,
the sensitivity levels and the score are unchanged.

**C38. The checks on the request run before the response cache.** The checks
on the request run first: a request they refuse gets their 403 and no cached
answer, and carries no `X-Agentcc-Cache-Status`. The checks on the answer
already read a cached answer (C25). The cache keys an entry by the request as
the checks left it, so two requests that differ only in what `pii-detection`
masks share one entry, and neither the entry's key nor its semantic vector is
built from the masked value. The checks on the request run after
authentication, RBAC and the request quota, and before the response cache and
the budget: a cache hit waits for them; an organization paused or over its
request limit is refused with 429 before any check reads its request, as
before; a request over budget is read by the checks before the budget refuses
it, so one that is also blocked gets the check's 403 rather than the
budget's 429. On the answer the order is the same: the checks on the answer
run before the cache stores it and before the budget records its cost. An
answer a check rewrites (`pii-detection` masking the answer) is stored as the
check left it, so a request the check does not apply to (one sent with
`X-Guardrail-Policy: disabled`, for example) is served the masked answer on
a hit; an answer a check withholds is not stored, in every cache backend. The
response cache is off on FutureAGI's hosted gateway (H2).

**C39. A check that rewrites the request and does not finish in time
refuses it.** A check runs with a timeout (a rule's `timeout` or
`guardrails.default_timeout`, an organization's `timeout_ms`). A check that
rewrites the request (`pii-detection` with `mask`, `redact` or `hash`, or an
organization's check with the action `mask`) runs on a copy of the request's
messages, which becomes the request only when the check finishes in time. When
it times out or fails, its rewrite is never applied, and the request is
refused before the provider is called with 403 `remediation_unavailable`
naming the check and saying why, with `guardrails.fail_open` on (the default)
or off, so the text the check is set to hide never reaches the provider.
Other checks keep their timeout behaviour, and each reads its own copy of the
request's fields, so one that outlives its timeout reads the request as it was
when it started, not a later check's rewrite or what the gateway sets after
the checks. A rule in `mode: async`, which the request does not wait for, runs
on a copy of what it reads: a rewriting rule there only logs, and never
rewrites the request (U14).

**C40. Request metadata cannot set the gateway's own keys.** Request
metadata (the `x-agentcc-metadata` header and the body's `metadata` field)
shares one map with the keys the gateway writes for billing, cost, telemetry,
routing and identity. Every name the gateway writes into or reads out of
request metadata is reserved unless it is listed below as yours, and a test
reads the gateway's source and fails on any name written there (a literal or a
constant) that is neither. A reserved name is
dropped from both channels, in any letter case: the request still runs,
your other dimensions still land, and the dropped name does not reach your
logs. Rename a dimension of your own that uses one.

- Reserved: keys that start with `route_reason.`, and `a2a_context_id`,
  `a2a_task_id`, `access_group_matched`, `access_groups`,
  `answer_unavailable`, `assistants_operation`, `audio_seconds`,
  `completion_tokens`, `complexity_original_model`, `complexity_override`,
  `complexity_provider`, `complexity_score`, `complexity_source`,
  `complexity_tier`, `edge_cache`, `edge_cache_control`, `edge_cache_etag`,
  `edge_cache_vary`, `endpoint_format`, `endpoint_type`,
  `failover_attempts`, `failover_original_provider`, `fallback_model`,
  `fallback_provider`, `images_generated`, `input_characters`,
  `input_token_warning`, `input_tokens`, `model_alias`, `model_deprecated`,
  `model_map_matched`, `model_map_provider`, `model_max_output_tokens`,
  `no_perf_disclosure`, `original_model`, `original_provider`,
  `output_tokens`, `privacy_mode`, `privacy_redacted`, `prompt_tokens`,
  `provider_lock_target`, `provider_locked`, `quality`, `race_winner`,
  `request_unavailable`, `response_content_type`, `response_size_bytes`,
  `routing_rule`, `routing_strategy`, `routing_strategy_applied`,
  `tools_filtered`, `tools_requested`, `translation_drops`,
  `vector_stores_operation` and `x-guardrail-policy`. C6 lists the other
  reserved names.
- Still yours, although the gateway uses them too: `provider_lock` (the
  metadata form of `x-agentcc-provider-lock`, checked like the header),
  `if_none_match` and `edge_cache_opt_in` (edge-cache requests about your
  own response), and `customer_id`, `license_id`, `instance_id`,
  `thread_id`, `message_id` and `assistant_id`. The gateway sets those six
  itself only under license authentication or on an Assistants route whose
  URL names one, and there its value replaces yours.
- `privacy_mode` in request metadata does not change how the request log or a
  trace export redacts the request. To keep less of a request in the log, send
  the `x-agentcc-log-content` header (N4).
- `X-Guardrail-Policy` is read only from its header (C18).
- A caller's `audio_seconds` never prices a transcription or translation
  and never reaches the request log: the gateway alone sets that key.

**C41. The gateway admin API refuses reserved key metadata on create.**
