`POST /-/keys` (behind the gateway's admin token) answers 400
`reserved_metadata`, naming them, as `PUT /-/keys/{id}` does for a change
(C6), to a key whose metadata holds `access_groups`, `role`, `team`, `type`,
`key_type`, `id`, `name` or `owner`, and makes no key. `org_id` and
`allow_policy_override` stay settable there, because the platform sets
them: it binds every key it makes to its organization, and its guardrail
playground key may override a policy. Both key-sync paths (`POST
/-/keys/sync` and the gateway's pull of the platform's keys) drop those
eight names from a key and keep the key. The platform never sends them, so
keys made in the console are unchanged. On a self-hosted gateway driven
through the admin API directly, give a key a role, a team or access groups
in `config.yaml` (`auth.keys[].metadata`), and rename a label of your own
that uses one of the eight names.

**C42. `/v1beta` checks the key before any handler.** With request auth on,
the native Google GenAI API (`/v1beta`) is gated at the edge as `/v1` is
(C2). A request with no key or an invalid one gets 401 `invalid_api_key`,
before any handler runs. The key is read as the GenAI handler reads it: the
`key` query parameter, then `x-goog-api-key`, then a `Bearer` Authorization
header. `x-api-key` is not read on `/v1beta`, as before. With license
authentication on, a managed service token gets 403 on `/v1beta` (it is valid
only for `POST /v1/chat/completions`), and a token that fails verification
gets 401, both before any handler.

**C43. `/v1/realtime` refuses every key but an internal one.** A key other
than an internal service key gets 403 `permission_denied` ("model ... is not
available for this API key") before a provider is resolved or a session slot
is taken. An internal key, and a gateway with request auth off, reach the
gateway's own provider and get 501: no provider in this release implements
realtime. Realtime through an organization's own providers is not in this
release.

**C44. File ids are random.** A file id is `file-` and 24 hexadecimal digits
from a cryptographic random source (96 bits). Treat a file id as opaque.

**C45. An organization's `default_max_tokens_estimate` is at least 1024.**
`managed_models.default_max_tokens_estimate` is the output a managed
request that sends no `max_tokens` is held for. Its minimum is 1024, a quarter
of the gateway's 4096 default (`managed.reserve_default_output_tokens`), so an
organization can lower its holds fourfold. A save below it (in
`managed_models`, or a rollout's) is refused with 400 naming the field. A
stored value below 1024 is pushed to the gateway as 1024 (the stored row is
not changed), and the gateway reads any lower value as 1024. A request that
sends `max_tokens` is held for that, with no floor. Managed models are
switched off in this release (Present but switched off).

**C46. An organization's own price for a model applies to the model that
served an own-key request, in either form.** An organization key's request
for `<provider>/<model>` that the organization's own provider serves now
reaches that provider's host as the id the organization registered,
`<model>`. The organization's own price for a model
(`cost_tracking.custom_pricing`) applies to such a request whether it is keyed
by the id asked or by the id registered; when both are keyed, the id asked
wins. A price keyed `<provider>/<model>` also prices a bare `<model>` request
that provider serves. It does not price the model when another provider serves
the request, with one exception: when a request asked for as
`<provider>/<model>` fails over to another provider, the failover takes that
price if its host answers naming the id it was sent, and its listed price if
its host answers with a dated id. A key that is both an id one of the
organization's providers registered and `<provider>/<model>`
(`anthropic/claude-sonnet-4` registered on an Anthropic-format router, say)
prices both. A model fallback hop, an auto-routed request's runner-up
included, is priced on the organization's price for its own model, in either
form, or else at its listed price, or unpriced when the catalog does not list
it; never at the price of the model asked for. A hop on a model the catalog
does not list, with no organization price, goes unpriced (no `x-agentcc-cost`,
`cost_source` `unpriced`). This changes the cost estimate (`x-agentcc-cost`,
with `cost_source` `org_custom`, `modeldb` or `unpriced`) of own-key requests
only: a managed request never takes the organization's prices.

**C47. An organization's API keys get its routing, over its own providers.**
A key limited to its organization's providers (every key made in the console)
gets the organization's retry, provider failover, circuit breaker and routing
strategy:

- `round_robin`, `weighted` (each provider's `weight`), `least_latency`,
  `cost_optimized` and adaptive routing choose among the organization's
  providers that list the model. With no strategy, the first of them in
  provider-id order serves, as in C48.
- Retry (`routing.retry`) retries the provider the request went to.
- Provider failover (`routing.failover.enabled`) moves a failed request to
  another of the organization's providers that lists the model, on
  `routing.failover.on_status_codes`. The model fallback switch alone does
  not move a request across providers; it moves it down its chain (C49).
- Circuit breakers (`routing.circuit_breaker`) are the organization's own:
  one circuit per provider and model. No other organization's failures and
  none of the gateway's own circuits can open them, and they open none of
  the gateway's. While every one of the organization's providers for the
  model has an open circuit, the request gets 503 without an upstream call.
- Strategy, retry, provider failover and circuit breakers keep such a key
  on the organization's own providers and keys. A model fallback is the
  one exception: when the organization turned managed models on, the next
  model of a chain can be a managed model, served by the gateway and
  billed to the organization's credits (with the managed credit check).
  With managed models off, a fallback also stays on the organization's
  providers.

A key of the gateway's own (`key_type: internal`) still fails over across
the gateway's providers. Two things change for an internal key of an
organization: the model map applies to it (C48), and when the
organization has chains and never set `fallback_enabled`, it runs the
organization's failover settings (C49).

**C48. The model map applies to every key of an organization.** A model
name the organization's `model_map` maps to one of its own providers
(enabled, with a key) goes only to that provider, as the organization's
own instance with the organization's credentials. This holds for an internal
key too. When that provider lists exactly one model and not the
requested name, the request is sent as that model, so a map entry can be
an alias. A map entry that names any other provider is not used for a key
limited to its organization's providers. A model with no map entry goes
to the first of the organization's providers that lists it in provider-id
order, unless, for a key limited to its organization's providers, a strategy
(C47) chooses among them. A chat request sends an alias as that model.
Embeddings, images, audio, rerank, OCR, search, `/v1/completions` and
`/v1/responses` take a mapped provider only when they can send the requested
name unchanged.

**C49. Model fallback chains run unless the organization turned them off.**
An organization whose `routing.model_fallbacks` holds a chain and that never
set `routing.fallback_enabled` now has its chains tried, on the failover
status codes (429, 500, 502, 503 and 504 unless it set others), as the
Fallbacks page always showed. `fallback_enabled: false` keeps them off: they
stay stored and are never tried. The Fallbacks page and Org Config >
Routing show the same value for the switch, and the Provider Failover
switch, the timeout switches and the default status-code lists show what
the gateway runs when they are unset. A chain is looked up by the
requested name first, so a `model_map` alias's own chain runs, streamed
or not. An internal key of an organization that has chains and never set the
switch runs the organization's failover settings (its `routing.failover`, else
3 attempts on the default status codes) across the gateway's providers, as
with `fallback_enabled: true`.

**C50. Run budgets, per-call cost caps and deadlines are enforced.** On chat
completions:

- **Run budget.** A call names its run with `x-agentcc-budget-scope` (or
  `x-agentcc-ledger-id`), else `x-agentcc-run-id`. `x-agentcc-budget-usd`
  sets that run's budget, shared by every call of the run in the same
  organization; a lower value sent later lowers it, a higher one is
  ignored. Before routing, each call holds its worst-case cost at the
  model's price (its prompt plus its `max_tokens`; with none, 4096 output
  tokens, or the model's maximum output when that is lower) and settles at
  what it cost. A call whose worst case is over what the budget has left
  has its `max_tokens` lowered to fit, as under a per-call cap (below);
  when not even 256 output tokens fit, or the budget is spent, it gets 402
  `agent_budget_exhausted` and is never sent upstream. `error.metadata`
  names the run (`scope: "run:<id>"`). Each call in a run with a budget
  answers `x-agentcc-budget-remaining-usd`, what the budget has left. A
  run's budget is kept in Redis
  for 24 hours after its last call (the org's
  `routing.agents.budget_scope_ttl_seconds`). A call that sends
  `x-agentcc-budget-usd` without a run id has a budget for that call alone.
  A retry that repeats the call's `x-agentcc-attempt-id` reuses its hold.
  Embedding and rerank calls that name a run count what they cost toward
  its budget too.
- **Per-call cap.** `x-agentcc-max-cost-usd` and
  `x-agentcc-budget-remaining-usd` cap one call, in a run or not; the lowest
  of them and the run's remaining budget applies. When the worst case is
  over the cap, `max_tokens` is lowered to the largest value that fits and
  the response says so in `x-agentcc-max-tokens-clamped`; when not even 256
  output tokens fit, the call gets 402 `agent_call_cap_exceeded` and is
  never sent. A model with no price cannot be held under a cap or a budget:
  402 `call_cap_unenforceable`, unless the org sets
  `routing.agents.unpriced_under_cap` to `allow`. A cap needs no Redis.
- **No Redis.** A run budget needs the gateway's Redis. Without it, a call
  that sends a run budget gets 503 `budget_state_unavailable`; with Redis
  configured but unreachable, every call that names a run gets it. An org
  that sets `budgets.fail_mode` to `open` is served instead, with nothing
  held.
- **Deadline.** `x-agentcc-deadline-ms` (also `x-omega-deadline-ms` and
  `metadata.agent.deadline_ms`) is counted from when the gateway received
  the call. On a non-streaming call every upstream attempt runs under it:
  an attempt still running at the deadline is cut and its upstream
  connection closed, and the call gets 504 `gateway_timeout`; an attempt
  that would start after the deadline is never sent and is logged as
  skipped (reason `deadline`).
  When no attempt could start before the deadline, the call gets 504
  `deadline_exceeded`, and every response the deadline ended carries
  `x-agentcc-deadline-exceeded: true`. A retry's wait never runs past the
  deadline: the call ends at it with the last attempt's error. A stream
  whose first chunk has not arrived by the deadline is answered the same
  way; a stream that has started is cut at the deadline and ends with a
  `gateway_timeout` error event and no `[DONE]`.

**C51. `GET /v1/capabilities` and `POST /v1/feedback` have fixed shapes.**
Both answer any valid key. Their field names and JSON types are pinned by
contract tests; a later release may add fields but does not rename, remove
or retype these. What `/v1/capabilities` lists (endpoints, providers and
their values) depends on the gateway's and the organization's providers.

- `GET /v1/capabilities` returns `generated_at` and `endpoints`, each with
  `endpoint`, `launch_tier` and `providers` (by provider id: `status`,
  `credential_type`, `features`, `managed_allowed`, `source`, `qualified`,
  `qualified_at`, and `price` when the provider has one: `sku`,
  `usd_per_unit_billed`, `markup_percent`). `features` holds the booleans
  `stream`, `tools`, `json_schema`, `vision`, `audio_input`, `reasoning`
  and `prompt_caching`. `GET /v1/capabilities?model=<id>` returns
  `generated_at`, `model` and `providers` (by provider id:
  `credential_type`, `source`, `flags` (the same booleans),
  `context_length`, `max_output_tokens`, `modalities` with `input` and
  `output`, and `endpoints` by endpoint with `status` and
  `managed_allowed`).
- `POST /v1/feedback` takes one JSON object of at most 4 KB:
  `generation_id` (the call's `x-agentcc-request-id`, at most 256 bytes),
  `outcome` (`ok`, `bad` or `verify_failed`) and an optional numeric
  `score`, and no other field. It answers 200
  `{"data": {"generation_id", "outcome", "event_id"}}`. Any other body gets
  400 `invalid_feedback`. An unknown generation, another organization's,
  one older than an hour, or one the gateway did not keep (a BYOK call
  with no agent headers) gets 404 `generation_not_found`.

**C52. Provider key rotation changes the key the gateway sends.** For a
provider in the gateway's config file with `rotation.enabled`:

- The promote first makes one upstream call with the new key: `openai` and
  `azure` providers list models; other formats, and an OpenAI-compatible
  server whose models endpoint answers 404 or 405, send a one-token chat
  completion to the first of the provider's `models`. A key the provider
  refuses answers 422 and stays pending; nothing changes. The answer gives
  the provider's status, type and code, never its message, since a provider
  may repeat the key it refused. A validation call that gets no answer
  (nothing listening, a TLS error, the provider's timeout) also answers 422
  and stays pending, saying the call got no answer and the key was not
  checked.
- From the promote on, every new call and every retry sends the new key.
  A call already in flight finishes on the old one, whose idle connections
  close after the provider's own `drain_period` (default 30 s). An async
  job (`x-agentcc-async: true`) queued before the promote and started after
  it sends the new key.
- With `redis` on, the promote reaches every replica over Redis pub/sub,
  the key sealed with a key derived from the admin token; nothing is
  stored. A replica that is not subscribed at that moment keeps its key.
- Rollback cancels a pending rotation. A promoted key already serves every
  call, so rolling it back answers 409. To go back, wait for the drain to
  end (`drain_until` in `GET .../rotation` and in the 409) and start a new
  rotation with the previous key; a rotation asked for while one drains
  answers 409 naming `drain_until`. An unknown provider, or one without
  `rotation.enabled`, answers 404.
- The rotation lives in the gateway's memory, and a restarted or newly
  rolled-out replica starts with the key in its deployment config: update
  the deployment config with the new key before the next restart or
  rollout. Every rotation answer and `GET .../rotation` say so
  (`applies_until: "restart"` and `note`).
- Each promote writes one log line naming the provider, where the key came
  from, how it was validated and the drain period; no log line carries a
  key or part of one.
- Bedrock providers are not rotated this way (they sign with AWS
  credentials); `rotation.enabled` on one is ignored with a warning.

**C53. Budget refusals name their level; Throttle has its own code.** Every
budget refusal (429 `org_budget_exceeded`, `budget_exceeded` or the new
`budget_throttled`) now carries, in the request log's metadata,
`budget_exceeded_level` (`org`, `team:<team>`, `user:<user>`,
`key:<key name>` or `tag:<key>:<value>`), `budget_period` (`daily`,
`weekly`, `monthly` or `total`), `budget_period_start` (RFC 3339, UTC) and
`budget_action` (`block` or `throttle`). A value the caller sent for any of
the four is overwritten. A Throttle budget past its limit answers 429
`budget_throttled` with `Retry-After` (F28).

**C54. A per-user budget is the request's user's.** The user level of an
organization's budgets counts the request's user: `x-agentcc-user-id`
(or `metadata.agent.user_id`) when sent, else the body's `user`, else the
key's owner. The key owner's own user budget also applies when
the request names another user;
one request is charged once to each of those users. A user id longer than
64 bytes keys its Redis counter by a hash.

**C55. An organization's Model Database overrides apply to its requests.** An org's Settings > Org Config > Model
Database overrides are merged on top of the gateway's model database for that org's requests. A field left blank keeps
the model database's value.

**Which entry applies.** An override keyed `gpt-4o` applies to `gpt-4o` and to `openai/gpt-4o`. An override keyed
`openai/gpt-4o` applies when the org's `openai` provider serves `gpt-4o`. The cost plugin, the validation plugin, model
"auto", `/v1/count_tokens`, `/v1/capabilities` and `/v1/models` all match entries this way.

**Prices: precedence.** Prices apply only to calls made with the org's own provider keys (BYOK):

1. An explicit Cost Tracking custom price (`cost_tracking.custom_pricing`, while cost tracking is on) wins.
2. Otherwise the Model Database override's input, output and cached-input prices apply, field by field. A blank field
   keeps the model database's price.
3. Otherwise the model database's price applies.

A call to a managed model is always billed at FutureAGI's managed catalog price. No org override changes a managed
price.

The Model Database tab shows no Batch Input/Output per Token fields: no gateway path prices a call at batch rates,
since gateway batch jobs call the provider's regular API. Stored values are kept as they are.

**Token limits** apply to every call of the org, BYOK and managed (they only narrow what the org's own traffic may do):

- **Max Output Tokens.** A request whose `max_tokens` or `max_completion_tokens` is above the limit is refused with
  400 `max_tokens_exceeds_limit`, and the message names the limit. A request that sets neither is sent with
  `max_tokens` set to the limit. We refuse instead of clamping because a silently shortened answer is worse than a
  clear error.
- **Max Input Tokens.** A request whose estimated input (about four characters per token) is above the limit is
  refused with 413 `input_too_large`, and the message names the limit. The org's limit is checked exactly, without
  the 1.5x margin the gateway allows on the model database's own limit.
- Under model "auto", the limits and switches of the model auto picks apply.
- `/v1/count_tokens` reports the org's Max Input Tokens as `max_context_tokens` and counts `remaining_tokens` against
  it.

**Capabilities.** Each capability is a three-way choice: Default, On or Off.

- **Off** refuses a request that uses the capability with 400 `model_capability_error`, and the message names the
  switch: Function Calling (tools), Parallel Tool Calls, Vision (an image part), Audio Input, Audio Output, PDF Input
  (a file part), Streaming, Response Schema (a `json_schema` response format), System Messages (a system or developer
  message) and Reasoning (`reasoning_effort`, `reasoning` or `thinking`).
- **On** lets through a request the model database would refuse.
- **Default** keeps the model database's value.
- Prompt Caching has no request-side signal. It changes only what `/v1/models` and `/v1/capabilities` report.

A key's `/v1/capabilities` view shows the org's limits and switches on every model cell and in the per-provider
rollup. Prices are not shown there. The admin view (`/-/capabilities`) and a global key's view are unchanged.

**C56. Model `auto` prices your own models as you pay for them.**

- **Own-provider candidates** are priced with the same precedence as the bill: Cost Tracking, then Model Database,
  then the gateway's model database. Auto ranks, and applies the price caps, at the price the call is billed at. The
  org's limits and switches count too.
- **Managed candidates** keep the catalog price. Only the org's limits and switches apply to them.
- **Preset provider ids** find their models under the model database's prefixes: `together` -> `together_ai/`,
  `fireworks` -> `fireworks_ai/`, `vertex` -> `vertex_ai/`, `google` -> `gemini/`, `x-ai` -> `xai/`. The provider's
  own `<provider>/<model>` entry is checked first, and the general entry last. `/v1/capabilities` uses the same
  lookup.
- **A model nothing prices** is still set aside, and the reason is recorded.
- **Console.** Under Models > Auto routing, How auto works states the price rule. In the candidate picker, each of
  your providers' models shows the price auto will use (Cost Tracking or Model Database), or says that auto skips it
  unless the gateway's model database prices it.

**C57. `/v1/models` lists only what the key can call.**

Each customer (non-internal) key lists and GETs only:

- the models of its org's own providers that hold credentials, narrowed by the key's provider and model ACLs and
  described as the org sees them (Model Database limits, prices and switches; Cost Tracking prices win);
- `auto`;
- the managed catalog, when the org has opted in (also under the key's ACLs), at FutureAGI's catalog prices with the
  org's Model Database token limits and capability switches applied, since those also apply to its managed calls.

No operator provider's model is listed. Any other id answers 404 `model_not_found`. A customer key without an org
lists only `auto`, because chat refuses it every operator model. Internal keys, license-authorized requests and
gateways without keys are not narrowed this way.

**C58. `route-hint` and `require` steer model `auto`.** They can be sent as headers
(`x-agentcc-route-hint`, `x-agentcc-require`, or the `x-omega-` forms) or as `metadata.agent.route_hint` and
`metadata.agent.require`.

**route-hint**

- `cost`, `balanced` and `quality` set auto's preference for the request when no `x-agentcc-auto-preference` header
  is sent. The header wins, and both win over the org's `routing.auto.preference`.
- `latency` is refused under model "auto" with 400 `route_hint_unsupported`, and the message names the value. Auto
  ranks by price tier against the task. The only latency data it has is this node's recent per-model P50, which a
  model lacks until it has been called on the node, and which also depends on output length. A "fastest" ranking
  built on it would be arbitrary on a fresh node, so a clear refusal is safer than a hint that silently does
  something else.
- On a named model, route-hint changes nothing. It is accepted and recorded, as before.

**require**

- Accepts `tools`, `json_schema`, `vision`, `reasoning`, `audio` and `long_context` (a context window of at least
  128,000 tokens), as a comma-separated list.
- Auto sets aside every candidate that lacks a required capability. Capabilities are read from the org's view of the
  model, so its Model Database switches count. The decision reason says "requires ...".
- If no candidate remains, the request is refused with 400 `no_auto_candidate`, and the message names the
  requirement.
- In the console, the Auto routing tab's Preference card names route-hint, and How auto works lists the require
  filter.

**C59. Analytics refuses a granularity it cannot bucket by.**
`usage-timeseries`, `latency-stats`, `error-breakdown` and
`guardrail-trends` now bucket by `5m`, `15m`, `1h`, `6h` or `1d` (fixed
widths, aligned to UTC) as well as `minute`, `hour`, `day`, `week` and
`month`; no granularity is `hour`. Any other value answers 400 naming the
granularity. The analytics endpoints that do not bucket (`overview`,
`cost-breakdown`, `model-comparison`, `guardrail-overview`, `guardrail-rules`)
still ignore it (F37).

**C60. Webhook payloads carry the error code.** Every webhook event's
payload has `error_code`: the code of the gateway's error answer for
the request, or `""`. It is `""` when that code can be a provider's own:
the openai and azure adapters pass a provider's code through, and an error
answered as `provider_<HTTP status>` with a nonzero status (the provider's,
or 502 for some upstream failures the gateway reports itself) is left out
with them. Other provider errors keep their `provider_...` code: the
errors the anthropic, gemini, bedrock and cohere adapters
parse, the speech relays' refusals, and a provider that never answered
(`provider_0`). A provider's code never raises `budget.exceeded`.
A `budget.exceeded` payload also has `budget_level`, `budget_period`,
`budget_period_start` and `budget_action`, each `null` when the gateway's
refusal did not name it (F35).

**C61. Email alert fields.** An email alert's `thresholds` are checked when
it is saved: `error_rate_percent` above 0 and at most 100, `latency_ms` a
whole number from 1, `cost_usd` above 0, `window_minutes` a whole number
from 1 to 1440 and `min_requests` a whole number from 1; anything else
answers 400 (other keys are stored as sent and never read). Reads add
`last_error` and `last_error_at`, the last failed delivery, cleared by the
next email sent. `last_triggered_at` is the last alert email sent; Send test
neither sets it nor waits for the cooldown (F34).

**C62. `x-agentcc-config` is read, and a config the gateway cannot apply is
refused.** The header is a JSON object of at most 16 KiB. Its keys are request
header names without `x-agentcc-`: `cache-ttl`, `cache-namespace`,
`cache-force-refresh`, `request-timeout`, `timeout`, `max-attempts`,
`retry-delay-ms`, `backoff`, `trace-id`, `session-id`, `session-name`,
`session-path`, `request-id`, `user-id`, `metadata`, `property-<name>`,
`provider-lock`, `complexity-override`, `auto-preference`,
`guardrail-policy`, `log-content` and the agent context fields (`run-id`,
`max-cost-usd`, ...). Each key sets its header unless the request sends that
header itself, and the value is then checked and applied as the header is:
a provider lock only to a provider the key may use, retry headers only
tighten the org's retries, a timeout only shortens. The Agent Command
Center SDK's nested GatewayConfig (`GatewayConfig.to_headers()`) is read
too: in its `cache` section, `ttl`, `namespace` and `force_refresh` apply
as the cache headers. The `retry`, `fallback`, `load_balance`,
`guardrails`, `routing`, `mirror` and `timeout` sections and the other
cache fields are not read; the response names them in
`x-agentcc-config-ignored` (for example `cache.strategy,retry`). A section
or field that is null asks for nothing. A string `timeout` is the
`x-agentcc-timeout` duration (`"30s"`); a numeric `timeout` is named there
and not applied. `cache-ttl` and `cache.ttl` take whole seconds or a
duration string (`"5m"`), and `request-timeout` whole milliseconds; a
number whose value is whole counts (`300`, `300.0` and `3e2` are all 300),
and a fraction such as `300.5` is refused. An unknown key, a value of
the wrong type, a section that is not an object, two keys that set the same
header, a duplicate key, more than 32 sections and cache fields to name, a
cache field name that is not 1 to 64 letters, digits, `_`, `-` or `.`, or
more than 16 KiB answers 400 `invalid_agentcc_config` naming it, before
authentication.

**C63. `x-agentcc-guardrail-policy` is read.** It is an alias of
`X-Guardrail-Policy`, the guardrail mode for the request (`log-only`,
`disabled` or `strict`), which the key must allow (`allow_policy_override`;
without it, 403). It takes a mode, not policy ids. When both are sent,
`X-Guardrail-Policy` wins. Where guardrail checks run for the request, a
request that sends policy ids in it is refused (403, or 400 `invalid_policy`
where the key allows overrides).

**C64. A provider's own client timeout answers 504.** When the HTTP
client timeout of an OpenAI-compatible, Azure or Bedrock provider (its
`default_timeout`) ends a call before the request's deadline, the request
gets 504 `gateway_timeout` ("provider request timed out"), as it does when
the request's deadline ends the call. The default retry, failover and
circuit-breaker lists include 504. A retry or failover list matches this
timeout when it names 504 or turns on `on_timeout`. A circuit breaker counts a
provider timeout (504, or a provider's 408 or 504) as a failure whatever its
`on_status_codes`. On an OpenAI-compatible provider, an answer whose body
stops arriving until a deadline ends it, or a stream whose first chunk does
not arrive before one, also gets 504 `gateway_timeout`; a provider that cannot
be reached at all still gets 502.

**C65. A request's own id is kept, and the gateway's stays.** A
client's `x-agentcc-request-id` (at most 255 characters) is logged as
metadata `client_request_id` and echoed as `x-agentcc-client-request-id`.
The response's `x-agentcc-request-id` is the gateway's own id. A longer value
answers 400 `invalid_agentcc_header`.

**C66. An organization's rate limits apply as Org Config describes them.**
The org config's `rate_limiting` (Org Config > Rate Limiting) counts and
limits the organization's chat completions, completions, Responses,
Anthropic Messages and Gemini (`/v1beta`) requests
(`/v1/messages/count_tokens` too, when an Anthropic-format provider serves
it):

- `global_rpm`: the organization's requests per minute on those routes,
  from every key together.
- `global_tpm`: the organization's tokens per minute on those routes, every
  key together.
- `per_key_rpm`: each key's requests per minute, as before. A limit set on
  the key itself (`auth.keys` `rate_limit_rpm`) is used instead.
- `per_key_tpm`: each key's tokens per minute.
- `per_model_rpm`: each model's requests per minute from every key of the
  organization, counted by the model name the request sends.
- `per_user_rpm`: each end user's requests per minute from every key. On
  chat completions the user is `x-agentcc-user-id` (or
  `metadata.agent.user_id`), else the body's `user`; on completions and
  Responses it is the body's `user`. Anthropic Messages and Gemini requests
  name no user here, so it does not limit them; nor a request that names no
  user: the key's owner, which budgets fall back to, is not a user here.

A relayed speech session (N12) counts as one request against
`global_rpm` and `per_key_rpm`, and against `per_model_rpm` by the model
the session's URL names; a session whose URL names no model (Cartesia
synthesis, or a session on the vendor's default model) is not counted per
model. A session over one of them is refused at the WebSocket handshake
with 429 naming it, before the vendor is reached. A session adds no
tokens to `global_tpm` or `per_key_tpm`, but while other requests have
spent one of them, or the key's own `rate_limit_tpm` (C75), for the
minute, a new session that limit holds is refused the same way.

A token limit lets a request through while the minute's
tokens are under the limit, and when the request finishes it counts the
tokens its response reports, streams included. While a request the gateway
reads as chat messages runs (chat completions and completions, and Messages
or Gemini requests it translates for a provider of another format), it
holds an estimate: its messages at about 4 characters a token plus
`max_completion_tokens` or `max_tokens`. A request that failed with no
answer counts none, and a stream that ended without reported usage keeps
its estimate. One request can therefore take the minute over the limit; the
next one is refused. A request is counted against every limit, or, when one
refuses it, against none.

The refusal is 429. On chat completions, completions and Responses it is
`rate_limit_exceeded`, with `error.param` naming the field that refused it
(`global_rpm`, `global_tpm`, `per_key_rpm`, `per_key_tpm`, `per_model_rpm`
or `per_user_rpm`; a key's own limit is `rate_limit_rpm`, the gateway's
default per key `rate_limiting.global_rpm`), a message naming the limit and
its value, and `Retry-After`; a refused key limit there now carries the
same `param` and `Retry-After`. On Anthropic Messages and Gemini it is that
API's own error (`rate_limit_error`, `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED`), whose message
names the field, its value and the seconds to wait. A successful chat
completions response carries `x-ratelimit-limit-requests`,
`x-ratelimit-remaining-requests` and `x-ratelimit-reset-requests` for the
tightest request limit and, when a token limit is set,
`x-ratelimit-limit-tokens`, `x-ratelimit-remaining-tokens` and
`x-ratelimit-reset-tokens`.

With Redis (`redis.enabled`), every replica shares the counts, and each
counter's key names its organization. A caller's `x-agentcc-metadata` or
body `metadata` cannot set or raise any of these limits. The gateway applies
them only while its own `rate_limiting.enabled` is on (the default); Org
Config > Rate Limiting shows whether this gateway reports rate limiting as
enforced.

**C67. `POST /-/batches` names the API key its items run as.** A batch
submit takes, next to the admin token, the header
`x-agentcc-batch-key: <an API key>` (bare or `Bearer <key>`). Each item runs
as a `POST /v1/chat/completions` request authenticated by that key, so it
runs as the key's organization: its providers, model access, guardrails,
budgets, rate limits and credits, and the request log files the item under
the key, its own request id and the batch request's trace id. An item's
client address is the submit's, so an organization or key IP allowlist
applies to the address that submitted the batch. With request auth on (every
shipped config), a submit without the header answers 400
`batch_key_required`, and an unknown, revoked or expired key 401
`invalid_api_key`; nothing runs. With auth off a submit may leave it out and
runs as before; a key it names is still checked. `GET /-/batches/{id}` and
`POST /-/batches/{id}/cancel` take `org_id`: a batch is found only with its
own organization's id (and a batch submitted without a key only with no
`org_id`); any other answers 404 and leaves the batch running. Submit, get
and cancel answer with `org_id`, and submit and get with `key_id`; the key
itself is never returned. The 10 MiB body limit and the default concurrency
(10, capped at the number of requests) are unchanged. Batches still live in
the gateway's memory: a restart loses them, and a finished batch is kept for
an hour. So batches need a single gateway replica: get and cancel answer 404
on any replica but the one that accepted the batch, and a key made with
`POST /-/keys` on one replica is unknown to the others until their next
control-plane key sync, so a submit naming it there answers 401
`invalid_api_key`.

**C68. An organization's alerts say how serious they are.** An alert rule
now carries a severity, `info`, `warning` or `critical`, set in Monitoring >
Create Rule and in Org Config > Alerting. The gateway sends it with every
alert the rule fires: as `severity` in the webhook's JSON body, and as a
"Severity:" line in the Slack message, whose emoji follows it. A rule saved
without a severity is sent as `warning`. An alert from a rule in the
gateway's own `config.yaml` has no severity, so its webhook body has no
`severity` field. `severity` is a field of an alert rule in the admin
contract.

**C69. An organization's provider health is the organization's own.**
`GET /agentcc/gateways/{id}/providers/` takes each provider's `status` from
the organization's request logs of the last 24 hours: `unhealthy` when 50%
or more of its requests failed, `degraded` at 10% or more, both counted
from 5 requests; with fewer it is `healthy`. A guardrail block (403 with a
triggered check) and a request the operator's managed models served are
not counted at all: not as a request, a failure or in the latency. `status_reason` says which rule applied (for example
`fewer than 5 requests in 24 h`), `healthy` is `status != "unhealthy"`, and
`circuit_state` is `"n/a"`: the status never reads the operator's monitor. A
provider the config push leaves out has `pushed: false`,
`status: "unhealthy"` and a reason such as
`not pushed: unsupported credential key organization` (C73) or
`not pushed: the stored credential cannot be decrypted`.

**C70. Only an Owner or Admin's health check re-pushes every organization's
config.** Any member may run `POST /agentcc/gateways/{id}/health_check/` and
gets the result. Its side effect, pushing every organization's active config
to the gateway (at most once per 5 minutes), runs only when an Owner or Admin
of the organization checks, and `org_configs_pushed` says whether this check
ran it. Every check, passed or failed, records its time and outcome for the
organization: list, retrieve, `health_check` and `config` return
`last_health_check` (ISO 8601, null when none is recorded) and
`last_health_check_status` (`healthy` or `unreachable`). The record is kept in
the platform's cache; after the cache is cleared it reads as no check recorded
until the next one.

**C71. `GET /agentcc/gateways/{id}/config/` carries the gateway's own
settings.** It now adds the `server`, `logging` and `batch` blocks and the
`org_sections_enforced` list of the gateway's `GET /-/config`, unchanged.
Nothing else from `/-/config` is copied; a block the gateway does not report
is absent, as it is when the gateway cannot be reached or refuses the read.

**C72. `test-playground` reads a guardrail outcome as the gateway sends
it.** In `POST /agentcc/gateways/{id}/test-playground/`, `blocked` is true
for a 403 whose `error.type` is `guardrail_error`, and `warned` for a 2xx
with `x-agentcc-guardrail-triggered: true`. The new `guardrail_action`
(`warn` or `log`) and `triggered_checks` (the check names) come from
`x-agentcc-guardrail-action` and `x-agentcc-guardrails-triggered`; they are
null and empty when the gateway does not send them.

**C73. A provider credential with a key the gateway does not take is
refused.** `POST /agentcc/provider-credentials/`, its `rotate` action and the
console's `update-provider` answer 400 naming each such key (for example
`organization` or `api_secret`). The legacy `access_key`, `secret_key` and
`region` still map to `aws_access_key_id`, `aws_secret_access_key` and
`aws_region`. A provider stored with such a key shows as not pushed (C69);
rotating its credentials without the key repairs it.

**C74. Request-log dates, export, guardrail feedback and custom property
defaults.**

- `started_after` and `started_before` on `GET /agentcc/request-logs/`, its
  search, sessions and export read an ISO 8601 offset (`Z` or `+05:30`; send
  `+` as `%2B` in a query string). A value with no offset is read as UTC,
  as before: it is never refused. The console now sends the offset, so its
  date filter matches the local times the table shows.
- `GET /agentcc/request-logs/export/` validates its filters as the list does:
  a value it cannot read answers 400. 0 matching rows answers 400
  `export_empty`; more than 10,000 answers 400 `export_too_large` with the
  count. A new `max_rows` (1 to 10,000) streams the first rows in the
  `ordering` sent (newest `started_at` first when none is sent) instead of
  that refusal. The
  console's Export shows the count and the 10,000-row cap before it
  downloads, and the server's message when it refuses. Times in the file
  are UTC.
- Guardrail feedback keeps one answer per request, check and reviewer:
  `GET /agentcc/guardrail-feedback/?mine=true` lists your own answers;
  `PATCH /agentcc/guardrail-feedback/{id}/` changes `feedback` and `comment`
  only (403 `feedback_not_yours` for someone else's answer, 400
  `feedback_target_fixed` for a change of request or check); a second POST
  for the same request and check updates your answer. The summary counts each
  reviewer's latest answer once.
- A custom property's Default is not applied anywhere (the gateway and the
  request logs never fill it in), so a new non-null `default_value` answers
  400 `property_rule_not_enforced`. A Default saved before stays readable
  and may be kept as it is or cleared with `null`. Required and Allowed
  Values are unchanged: `POST /agentcc/custom-properties/validate/` checks
  them; the gateway does not apply them to requests.

**C75. A config-file key's `rate_limit_tpm` limits its tokens.** A key
defined in the gateway's config file (`auth.keys`) is held to its
`rate_limit_tpm`: once the tokens its answers reported in the current
minute reach it, the key's next request gets 429
`token_rate_limit_exceeded`. Checking the limit charges nothing; each
answer's reported tokens are charged when it finishes, so one request can
take the key past its limit, and an answer served from the response cache
is charged nothing. This window is the key's own, apart from its request
limit, and is counted on each replica (Known limits). Keys made through the
platform cannot carry it (C22); the organization's `global_tpm` and
`per_key_tpm` (C66) hold every key of the organization, one without a
limit of its own included.

**C76. A routing alias sends its target's model and keeps its fallback
chain.** When the model a request names has routing targets
(`routing.targets`) whose target sets `model_override`, the provider
receives that override, streamed or not, whether or not the alias is also
listed among the provider's models, and an alias with a single target is
routed like one with several. A `model_fallbacks` chain configured under
the alias's name runs when its targets fail. A single-target alias whose
only provider is unhealthy is refused, and nothing is sent upstream.

**C77. Chat answers keep the provider's own fields; Gemini runs its own
tools.** A chat completion, streamed or not, keeps every field the
provider sent that the gateway's response does not name (for example
Perplexity's `citations` and `search_results`, or a provider's own `usage`
counters); a field the gateway names keeps the gateway's value. On a
Gemini provider:

- a tool Gemini runs itself, written as `{"google_search": {}}` (or
  `googleSearch`, `url_context`, `code_execution`,
  `google_search_retrieval` with its config as written), reaches Gemini
  beside the caller's functions;
- a grounded answer that is not streamed carries its sources as
  `url_citation` annotations on the message, beside its
  `grounding_metadata`;
- a tool Gemini cannot serve is refused with 400 `unsupported_tool` before
  the provider is called, unless the request also has a tool Gemini
  serves: then only the served tools are sent, so a model alias or failover
  group that spans providers keeps working.

**C78. An error message carries no credential from a URL.** In an error
the gateway answers in the OpenAI, Anthropic or Gemini error format, a
URL's password and its credential query parameters (`key`, `api_key`,
`apikey`, `access_token`, `token`, `password`, `secret`, `sig`,
`signature`, `x-goog-api-key`, `subscription-key`) read `REDACTED`; the
rest of the message is unchanged. A Gemini API key travels in the
`x-goog-api-key` header, never in the URL. It is not sent across a redirect
to another origin, on any later hop of it included, and a redirect from
`https` to `http` is refused. Every provider format the gateway supports
is tested to keep its key out of its errors.

**C79. A stream carries its usage once, where the request asks for it.**
Streams on `/v1/chat/completions` and `/v1/completions` guarantee:

- A request that sets `stream_options: {"include_usage": true}` gets
  OpenAI's contract: exactly one chunk with empty `choices`, right before
  `[DONE]`. It carries the billed usage, and on a managed key the billed
  cost.
- A request that sets neither `stream_options.include_usage` nor the
  `x-agentcc-include-metadata` header gets no chunk with empty or null
  `choices`, so the usual loop over `chunk.choices[0].delta.content`
  reaches `[DONE]`. The billed usage rides on the last chunk that has
  choices, the one carrying `finish_reason`, as Mistral's API sends it.
  The gateway holds that finish chunk until the provider's stream has
  ended and the request is metered (the cost, the credits, the request
  log and an organization's output checks run first), then writes it with
  the usage. With `n` above 1 it holds each choice's finish chunk and puts
  the usage on the last one it writes. No other chunk waits, except the
  other choices in a chunk that carries a finishing choice: that chunk is
  held whole.
- `x-agentcc-include-metadata: 1` (or `true`) asks for the one final chunk
  as well, on both endpoints, and that chunk then carries
  `agentcc_metadata` beside the usage.
- On `/v1/chat/completions`, a managed stream whose provider sent no usage
  is billed on the gateway's estimate, and that estimate, with the billed
  cost, is the usage the client sees, in either shape.
- The usage a provider sends is metered and never forwarded, on whatever
  chunk it rides: its usage chunk, its finish chunk, Bedrock Converse's
  metadata chunk (one empty choice and the usage, after the finish chunk)
  or every chunk (Gemini). The client gets the usage once, where the
  first two bullets put it.
- A stream that ends at the request's deadline or on a provider error
  keeps its error event. A finish chunk the gateway held goes out just
  before that event, with the usage. A stream that a blocking check holds
  and blocks carries no chunk of the answer, its finish chunk included
  (C25). A disclaimer goes out when the provider's stream ends, and the
  held finish chunk
  follows it. When the request's timeout (`x-agentcc-request-timeout`,
  `x-agentcc-timeout`, the organization's or the model's timeout) ends a
  stream after the provider's finish chunk, the held finish chunk still
  goes out, with the usage when one is known by then, and the stream
  ends there without `[DONE]` or an error event.
- A chunk with empty or null `choices` that a provider sends anywhere in a
  stream (its usage chunk, or Azure's first chunk with
  `prompt_filter_results`) is metered and not forwarded, so the fields
  only it carries, such as Azure's `prompt_filter_results`, do not reach
  the client.

The gateway still asks the provider for usage on every chat stream, and
meters, logs and bills every stream on it the same way, whatever the client
asked. The `agentcc` 1.0.0 and `@agentcc/client` 1.0.0 SDKs never set
`stream_options`. They get the usage on the finish chunk:
`get_final_completion().usage`, `getFinalCompletion().usage` and the
`usage` stream event carry it. Two limits: a provider stream that never
sends a `finish_reason` gives a client that did not ask no usage, because no
chunk can carry it; and on `/v1/completions` a client that did not ask gets
the usage when the provider sends it. Streams on `/v1/messages`, `/v1beta`
and `/v1/responses` keep their own event formats.

**C80. The Assistants, Vector Stores and Files APIs run the gateway's
pipeline.** Each request on these APIs runs the pipeline as a request to a
model endpoint does:

- **A key's allowed models and access groups** apply to the four requests
  that name a model: creating and updating an assistant (its model), and
  creating a run or a thread and run (the run's model override). A key that
  may not use the model gets chat's answer for the same key and model,
  before the provider is called: 403 `model_not_permitted` with
  `metadata.reason` `key_model_denied` for its `allowed_models`, and chat's
  403 `permission_denied` for a model its access groups do not hold. An
  alias the key's access groups define is sent as the model it stands for.
  A key's allowed providers applies as C1 says.
- **The provider gets the model the gateway checked.** The gateway reads
  `model` as OpenAI reads it and sends that one `model`, and no other. Only
  an `openai/` prefix, in any letter case, is stripped (`openai/gpt-4o` is
  sent as `gpt-4o`), as chat strips the prefix that names the provider it
  routes to. Any other name with a `/` (another prefix, as in
  `anthropic/claude-x`), and `openai/` alone, names no model OpenAI serves:
  it gets chat's 403 `permission_denied` for a model no provider serves,
  before the provider is called. A body chat refuses as invalid JSON gets
  chat's 400 `invalid_json` on those four requests and on submitting tool
  outputs, before the provider is called. An empty body goes on as `{}`.
- **Rate limits**: the key's own `rate_limit_rpm` (for a key without one,
  the limit its organization's `rate_limiting` section or the gateway's
  `global_rpm` gives it) and its `rate_limit_tpm` apply, as on embeddings,
  images, audio and OCR. A request over the request limit gets 429
  `rate_limit_exceeded`. Once the key has spent its `rate_limit_tpm` for the
  minute, a request gets 429 `token_rate_limit_exceeded`. A refused request
  never reaches the provider. The organization's `rate_limiting` section
  counts the routes C66 names.
- **Request log**: each request the pipeline runs for an organization's API
  key has a request log entry, with the status the client got, the model it
  names (none for a list, a retrieval or a file) and its operation
  (`assistants_operation`, `vector_stores_operation` or `files_operation`).
  That includes run polls (`GET /v1/threads/{id}/runs/{id}`), lists and
  retrievals, and a request the pipeline refuses (a rate limit, a check on
  the request). A streamed run is logged when its stream ends.
- **Each of these requests is a metered gateway request.** As on the model
  endpoints, each request log entry becomes one `gateway_request` usage
  event, and these requests count toward the organization's monthly
  `gateway_requests` quota; a paused or over-quota organization is refused
  on them. An SDK helper that polls a run (`create_and_poll`) makes one
  metered request per poll.
- **Managed credentials never serve them.** The Assistants and Vector
  Stores APIs are served with the organization's own OpenAI key or, for an
  internal key and on a gateway with auth off, the operator's. When the
  gateway's `openai` provider is managed (`managed: true`), they answer 501
  `not_supported` to every key, an internal one included, before any
  provider call. The Files API calls no provider.

### New

**N1. Attempts in Request Logs.** Each upstream try is recorded as an
attempt, with its provider, model, outcome, price source and billing mode.
The Request Logs drawer lists a request's attempts, and the column picker
has an **Agent & billing** group. Attempts follow the content mode (N4). The
records come from new database tables (U6). Run ids show as plain text,
because the Runs page is not in this release (N7).

**N2. `model: "auto"`.** On `/v1/chat/completions`, send
`"model": "auto"` and the gateway chooses a model from the providers your
key can reach. It first sets aside models that cannot serve the request:
missing tool, structured-output or image support, a context or output limit
that is too small, a price above your caps, an open circuit, or the key's
model and access-group limits. It then scores the prompt as a simple,
standard or complex task and ranks the rest by how well their price tier
fits that task and your preference (`cost`, `balanced` or `quality`). The
next two models in the ranking become fallbacks. The response headers
`x-agentcc-auto-model` (the model auto chose, before any fallback),
`x-agentcc-auto-tier` and `x-agentcc-auto-reason` explain the choice, and
the request log records the top five of the ranking.
`x-agentcc-auto-preference` overrides the preference for one request. Set
the org defaults in the console, on **Gateway > Models > Auto routing**, or
in the org config's `routing.auto` (`preference`, `candidates`,
`max_input_per_mtok`, `max_output_per_mtok`).
If no model qualifies, the answer is 400 `no_auto_candidate`, with the
reasons.

Auto ranks the models your providers serve by how well their price tier
fits the task and your preference. No benchmark shows that it picks better
models than a fixed choice, and its scoring may change between releases.
[Known limits](#known-limits) says what else moves its ranking today.

**N3. Retry headers.** `x-agentcc-max-attempts` (tries per target, the first
call included: 1 to 5), `x-agentcc-retry-delay-ms` (0 to 60,000) and
`x-agentcc-backoff` (`constant`, `linear` or `exponential`) tune one
request's retries. They can only tighten the org's own retry settings,
never loosen them. A malformed value gets 400 `invalid_retry_header`.

**N4. Content mode and retention (API only).**

- The content mode decides what the gateway's request log keeps: `full`
  (today's behaviour, and the default), `metadata` (no request or response
  bodies and no request headers) or `none` (as `metadata`, and also no error
  text, no guardrail messages and none of your own metadata keys; attempts
  keep only their error class and status). Requests are still logged and
  billed in every mode.
- Set it per organization (`privacy.content_mode` in the org config), per
  key (the key's `log_content` field) or per request (the
  `x-agentcc-log-content` header). The most restrictive of these and the
  gateway's `privacy.default_content_mode` wins, and an unknown value counts
  as `none`.
- It governs the request log only. It does not change what reaches the
  provider, what the response cache stores, or what OpenTelemetry span
  export carries when `otel.include_bodies` is on. It is not a
  zero-data-retention mode.
- `privacy.retention.request_log_days` and `privacy.retention.attempt_days`
  (1 to 36,500) set how long request logs, outcomes and attempts are kept. A
  daily job (`agentcc-log-retention`) deletes older rows. With no value set,
  rows are kept, as today. Managed-credit ledger entries are never deleted.
- There is no console page for these settings yet.

**N5. Policy rollouts (API only).** The org config's `routing.rollout`
sends a percentage of traffic to a candidate routing config, sticky by
session, key or request. Each request log records the policy version and
whether the request took the stable or the candidate config.

**N6. Routing strategies for routes in the gateway's config file.**
`cost_optimized` now ranks targets by catalog price when no model database
prices them, and `adaptive` weighs each target by that model's own health.

**N7. Console.**

- **Gateway > Models.** On a deployment that offers no managed models (the
  hosted config for this release offers none, H1), the page shows no
  managed-models card and one tab, **Auto routing**. Its subtitle reads:
  Tune how model "auto" chooses between the models your providers serve. A
  link to the catalog tab opens Auto routing. Where managed models are
  offered, the page adds the managed card and a **Catalog** tab, which opens
  first.
- **Hidden pages.** Playground, Capabilities, Policies, Runs, Billing &
  Credits and MCP Tools are not in the navigation, and their URLs open the
  not-found page. The gateway connects only the MCP servers in its own
  config file, never an organization's, so the console has no MCP page, and
  the Org Config editor has no MCP tab: a save there never sends, rewrites
  or drops a stored `mcp` section.
- **Guardrails.** The Overview's Stage column shows where the gateway runs
  each check: After LLM only for data leakage and hallucination checks, the
  two that read the answer (C25). When a listed check reads the
  answer, a note says on which requests it does (chat, completions,
  Anthropic Messages, Gemini and Responses, streamed or not, C30), that a
  stream such a check reads in block mode is held until the check passes
  and a blocked stream carries none of the answer (C25), and that a
  background Responses request is refused when such a check would block it
  (C31). The edit dialog offers no stage. The Rules dialogs offer no
  DynamoAI Guard `MODEL_RESPONSE` text type, Tool Permissions Apply To, MCP
  Security Validate Outputs, Aporia Policies or IBM Granite Guardian
  groundedness criterion: those checks read the request only. Aporia's
  description says it checks the
  request with the Aporia project's guardrails. Saving a check again
  changes only the fields the admin edited. The dialog gives the stored
  check back as stored apart from those fields: settings the form does not
  show (such as these), settings it shows with a default when absent, text
  it would trim, and the keys' order. A guardrails section an older console
  stored as a checks map stays that map, and update-config gets only the
  checks the admin changed, so a check stored there without an action or a
  threshold still runs as the gateway ran it: as log, at threshold 0. A
  section stored as rules, or as the guardrail policy sync's list, is saved
  as rules, as before, and a check stored there without an action or a
  threshold keeps the defaults it runs with (block, 0.8).
- **Providers > Routing.** "Retry on Status Codes" and "Retry on Timeout" save
  the retry settings (`routing.retry.on_status_codes`,
  `routing.retry.on_timeout`). "Failover on Status Codes" saves
  `routing.failover.on_status_codes`. Each list says what it drives. Which
  upstream statuses a list matches is in F38.
- **Routing strategy (Providers > Routing, Org Config > Routing).** An
  unset `routing.strategy` shows as "None: first provider by ID", what the
  organization's keys run (C47, C48). Choosing None clears a stored strategy.
  Providers > Routing saves the strategy only when it is changed.
- **Guardrails tab: Fail Open and Mode (Org Config > Guardrails).** The two
  controls are read-only and show how this gateway runs an organization's
  checks, from `GET /-/config` `guardrail_pipeline` (N8): "Set by this
  gateway, read-only: it runs your checks in turn and stops at the first
  block, and a check that errors or times out blocks the request [lets the
  request through]. This organization's own Mode and Fail Open are saved and
  not applied." (or that guardrails are off on this gateway). Stored values
  are kept; Timeout (ms) stays editable. The Guardrails analytics tab shows no
  Avg Latency card: the gateway records no per-check latency.
- **Providers > Cache.** The Cache Configuration card shows only Enabled and
  Default TTL, what the gateway applies for an organization's cache, and
  says when this gateway runs no response cache ("Off on this gateway") or
  does not report it; stored values of other cache settings are kept.
- **Managed models and auto routing.** A save the platform stored and the
  gateway did not apply (`gateway_synced: false`) shows "Saved, but the
  gateway did not apply it: <reason>" as a warning, never the "Managed
  models enabled" or "Auto routing saved" toast.

**N8. `GET /-/config` reports the gateway's settings and the org
sections it enforces.** Besides `server.port`, `server.host` and the
counts, the answer (admin token) carries, with no secret:

- `server`: `read_timeout_ms`, `write_timeout_ms`, `idle_timeout_ms`,
  `max_request_timeout_ms`;
- `logging`: `level`, `request_logging`, `include_bodies`, `content_mode`
  (`privacy.default_content_mode`, `full` when unset);
- `batch`: `storage: "memory"`, `default_max_concurrency: 10` (what a
  submission without its own `max_concurrency` gets) and
  `max_body_bytes: 10485760` (the submit body cap);
- `org_sections_enforced`: a sorted list drawn from `alerting`, `budgets`,
  `cache`, `ip_acl`, `rate_limiting` and `tool_policy`, each present only
  when this gateway installed the plugin that reads it (`cache` only with
  the gateway's own cache, `rate_limiting` only with `rate_limiting.enabled`).
  A section missing from the list does nothing on this gateway;
- `org_sections_not_enforced`: a section this gateway does not enforce for
  a reason the console states, with that reason: `ip_acl` on a gateway with
  neither `server.trusted_proxies` nor `server.trusted_hops` is
  `"client IP unknown (trusted_proxies not set)"`; absent when there is
  none;
- `guardrail_pipeline`: how this gateway runs an organization's guardrail
  checks: `enabled` (`guardrails.enabled`), `fail_open` (the gateway's
  `guardrails.fail_open`, which an org check's error or timeout follows)
  and `mode` (`sequential`: checks run in turn and the first block stops
  the rest). An organization's own `fail_open` and `pipeline_mode` are
  stored and not read yet; the console's Guardrails tab shows these values
  read-only in their place.

**N9. Request properties and the session name and path are logged.**
`x-agentcc-property-<name>: <value>` is `"<name>": "<value>"` in
`x-agentcc-metadata` (the name lower-cased, at most 64 characters; the value
at most 1,024): it is logged and exported as one of your dimensions on every
route that reads metadata, under the same blocklist, so a property never
sets `org_id`, `key_type`, `cost` or another gateway-owned key.
`x-agentcc-session-name` (at most 255 characters) and
`x-agentcc-session-path` (at most 1,024) are logged as `session_name` and
`session_path`. A property wins over the same key in `x-agentcc-metadata`,
and `x-agentcc-request-id`, `-session-name` and `-session-path` win over a
property of their key. A longer value answers 400 `invalid_agentcc_header`. Browsers may send these
headers: CORS allows every documented request header, and a preflight that
asks for property headers is answered with their names.

**N10. `x-agentcc-latency-ms` on every response.** Errors (a refused
key, an unknown model, a bad body, an unknown route), embeddings,
`/v1/messages` and the models list now carry it beside
`x-agentcc-request-id` and `x-agentcc-trace-id`. `x-agentcc-provider` and
`x-agentcc-model-used` are on responses a provider served
(`x-agentcc-model-used` not on embeddings), and `x-agentcc-timeout-ms` where
the route resolved a timeout (not on `/v1/messages`).

**N11. Guardrail outcome headers.** When a check triggered without
blocking, the answer carries `x-agentcc-guardrail-action` (`warn` when any
check warned, else `log`; a check that rewrote the request counts as `log`)
and `x-agentcc-guardrails-triggered` (the checks' names, sorted,
comma-separated), beside `x-agentcc-guardrail-triggered: true`. A block
stays 403 with error type `guardrail_error` and carries neither. Browsers
can read both.

**N12. Deepgram and Cartesia streaming speech, relayed.** The gateway
serves each vendor's own WebSocket API under the vendor's name:
`/deepgram/v1/listen` and `/deepgram/v1/speak`, `/cartesia/tts/websocket`,
`/cartesia/stt/websocket` and `/cartesia/stt/turns/websocket`. Point the
vendor's SDK at the gateway's base URL and give it a gateway key, which the
gateway reads wherever that SDK sends one (`Authorization: Token` or
`Bearer`, `X-API-Key`, or the `api_key` query parameter). The gateway
connects to the vendor with the provider configured for it
(`api_format: deepgram` or `cartesia`, see `config.example.yaml`), sends
the vendor that provider's key and never the client's, and relays frames
unchanged.

- With request auth on (`auth.enabled`), only an internal key opens a
  session, since the platform's credential serves it: any other key gets
  403 and a request without a valid key gets 401, before the vendor is
  reached, and the key's IP allow list applies. With request auth off the
  gateway checks no key on any route, so any caller that reaches it opens
  a session on the platform's credential. A request from a browser page
  (one with an `Origin` header) gets 403 either way.
- The key's model list and access groups apply to the model in the URL
  and, on Cartesia synthesis, to the model each request names. A session
  whose URL names no model, and every Cartesia synthesis request with
  text that names none (also after an earlier request of the session
  named one), leave the model to the vendor: a key with a model list is
  refused them (403, or the request is refused and the connection
  closed), and access groups admit them only when one of the key's groups
  admits every model of that vendor (`*`, or a pattern such as
  `deepgram/*`).
- A query that names `model`, `encoding`, `sample_rate` or `channels`
  more than once gets 400 `ambiguous_parameter` before the vendor is
  reached, so the vendor receives only the values the gateway checked and
  metered. Other parameters may repeat and travel as sent.
- Usage is metered from what the client sends: seconds of raw audio for
  transcription (so a transcription needs a raw `encoding`, a
  `sample_rate` and, for more than one channel, `channels`, or gets 400
  `unmeterable_audio`), and characters of text for synthesis. It is priced
  at the session's model's price in the model database, per second or per
  character. A session whose model has no price, or that names no model,
  is recorded unpriced (`cost_source` `unpriced`), with no cost.
- A session counts against the organization's rate limits as C66 says
  and, once open, keeps relaying past the request timeout ceiling (C23)
  until either side closes it or the relay's own idle and session limits
  end it: a side that sends neither a message nor a ping answer for 10
  minutes ends it, and no session runs longer than 2 hours.

### Guarantees

**F1. Every request has its own request id.** Every request and trace id comes
from one crypto-random source per gateway process, and ids made in the same
millisecond never repeat. Managed credit holds and settlements are keyed on
the request id, and the platform's request log keeps one entry per request id.

Work the gateway starts itself gets ids from the same source: an A2A task,
each `/-/batches` item and each run of a scheduled job (which this build does
not run, U4), a scheduled job's retry included, has its own request id and
trace id. An A2A task's run carries the trace id of the A2A request that
started it (the `x-agentcc-trace-id` on that request's response), a task sent
with `returnImmediately` too, but a request id of its own, not that request's
`x-agentcc-request-id`. A `/-/batches` item's run
carries the batch request's trace id the same way, and each result of
`GET /-/batches/{id}` names the item's own `request_id`, the id its entry in
the request log is filed under.

**F2. A starting gateway reports ready only once it knows the keys made in
the app.** When the gateway syncs keys from a control plane
(`control_plane.url` with `sync_on_startup` or a `sync_interval`, as Helm and
docker-compose set it), `/readyz` now answers 503 until its first key sync
has loaded the keys made in the app. `/healthz` and `/livez` answer 200 from
the start. The wait reaches only a readiness probe that reads `/readyz`, as
the Helm chart's does: a probe on `/healthz` or `/livez` still sends traffic
to a replica that has not loaded the keys, so point it at `/readyz`. A gateway
with no control plane, or whose control plane syncs no keys (no
`sync_on_startup` and no `sync_interval`), is ready at once, as before:
nothing would end the wait. If the app cannot be reached when a replica
starts, that replica stays out of rotation until it can; replicas already
serving are not affected. With `sync_on_startup` off and a `sync_interval`,
the first key sync, and so readiness, comes one interval after start. The
first list can be empty (the app lists no key until one is made): it counts,
also on a replica that already holds a key made on it (`POST /-/keys`) or
pushed to it one by one (`POST /-/keys/sync`) before the list came; an empty
list keeps such a key.
An app whose database has no key table yet (a new install before its
bootstrap job has migrated it) answers the key list with 503
`migrations_pending`, and that counts as an empty first list: no key has been
made. So a Helm install with bundled datastores finishes under
`helm install --wait`, which runs the post-install bootstrap job only once the
gateway reports ready. The answer never changes the keys a replica holds, and
any other failed answer keeps the replica out of rotation.

**F3. `/v1/responses` streams are priced.** A `/v1/responses` stream is priced
from the usage its last event reports (`response.completed`, or
`response.incomplete` or `response.failed` for a response that stopped early),
as the same response is priced when it is not streamed, and the request takes
the model that event names. A `/v1/responses` request with `background: true`
that would be paid from prepaid credit (a managed key's own balance) is
refused before the provider is called, with 400 `usage_unavailable`, streamed
or not. A background response goes on after its stream or its queued answer,
and its usage never passes through the gateway, so it cannot be priced.
Send it without `background`. The refusal comes after the key is
authenticated, as the gateway's other refusals of a request do: a managed key
without access to the model gets 403, not this 400, and a revoked or expired
key gets 401 from the key check in front of every `/v1` route, as before.
Organization managed credits do not reach `/v1/responses` in this release
(managed models serve chat completions only); the refusal covers them too.
The gateway reads a stream's last event within 10 MiB of line. A stream
paid from prepaid credit whose last event is longer (a `response.completed`
that carries base64 images can be) has no usage the gateway can read: it is
charged what a hold for it is, never 0. That is its prompt as the gateway
estimates it and its `max_output_tokens`, or without one the most a hold
assumes (the model's maximum output, at most the organization's
`default_max_tokens_estimate` or the gateway's
`managed.reserve_default_output_tokens`, 4096 by default), priced at the
model's card as any usage is, so the request's cost, which the request log
records, is the amount the managed key is debited. The log marks
the request `usage_estimated`, with `usage_unreadable:
final_event_over_limit` in its metadata, and the gateway logs a warning.

**F4. The Guardrails page edits a section stored as a checks map.** An older
console stored an organization's guardrails as a checks map (N7). On such a
section, the Overview's switch and its edit dialog change the check in the
map. The switch sets its `enabled`. The dialog sends only what the admin
changed (`action` or `confidence_threshold`), and every other setting the
check is stored with is kept. A check the switch turns on that the map does
not hold yet is added with block and 0.8, as a new rule is. The Rules tab and
the Overview's edit dialog show a check of such a map stored without an action
or a threshold as the gateway runs it, log at threshold 0, and choosing Block
saves it as an edit. A section stored as rules, or as the guardrail policy
sync's list, is shown and saved as rules.

**F5. An organization guardrail config never reads the gateway's
environment.** A guardrail built from organization config takes every
credential, URL and setting exactly as written. The one environment
value it still reads is `FI_BASE_URL`, the operator's address for a
`futureagi` check that names no `base_url`, which is the operator's setting,
not the organization's, and holds no secret. A `${` in a `futureagi` check's
`api_key`, `secret_key` or `base_url`, or in any top-level setting of an
external-provider check, is a configuration fault: no call is made, and with
action `block` the request gets 403 `content_blocked` naming the check and
the field, never the value (as C3). With action `warn` or `log` the failure
is warned or logged, and the request goes on.

The console and the control plane refuse such a value first, with a 400
that names the field: on every save that sends a guardrail check (org
configs in each shape, update-config, update-guardrail, guardrail policies),
on a save that would switch on a check whose stored value holds one or
change anything else about such a check (its endpoint, provider or action,
say), and on activating a version with such a check switched on. A save
that pushes such a check exactly as the active version does goes ahead (the
check itself keeps failing closed at the gateway until it is fixed), and
switching a check off is never refused for the value it holds. Where the
switch is depends on how the organization's guardrails are stored: U10
lists each case, including a config written by a guardrail policy (F15). A guardrail
policy's check config must be a JSON object: a string, list, number or
boolean answers 400 (an absent or null config still means none). Anywhere
else in a guardrail config (a blocklist word such as `${name}`, a pattern, an
expression, a webhook header, a check name) `${` is plain text: it saves, and
the gateway runs it as written. A save that does not change guardrails (a
budget, an MCP server) is never refused. A rule in the gateway's own
`config.yaml` is operator-trusted and still expands `${VAR}` (U5).

When a `futureagi`, external-provider or webhook check's call fails, its
message carries neither the URL nor the address the gateway dialed; the
gateway's log keeps the full error. Before upgrading a self-hosted gateway,
see U10.

**F6. The request log never stores a credential a caller sends in a
header.** The request log keeps the name of every credential
header with the value `***`: the headers the gateway reads a key from, the
headers a provider or guardrail service takes a key in, and any header
whose name reads as a credential (a token, a secret, a password, an API
key, a cookie, a header whose last word is `key` or `auth` such as
`X-OpenAI-Key`, `x-key` or `Helicone-Auth`, and every `X-Portkey-` header).
A name names a token when `token` is in any of its words (`X-Access-Token`,
`X-Access-Token-V2`, `slack_token_prod`, `X-CSRFToken`), except where
`token` is followed by `s` (a count of tokens, such as `prompt_tokens` or
`X-Ratelimit-Remaining-Tokens`), by `iz` (a tokenizer: `Tokenizer`,
`tokenize`), or by `-` or `_` and one of `count`, `budget`, `limit`,
`usage`, `used`, `remaining`, `total`, `max` or `min` (such as
`X-Token-Count` or `token_budget`). A last word `key` after a word that
names a lookup key (`cache`, `idempotency`, `dedup`, `dedupe`,
`deduplication`, `partition`, `sort`, `routing`, `lookup`, `primary`,
`foreign` or `public`, as in `Idempotency-Key` or `cache_key`) is not one
either, and neither is a bare `key`. The trace export never exports them
either. The platform masks them again on its own, in `request_headers` and
`response_headers`, whatever a gateway sends, and stores headers only when
they are sent as an object. A request's detail shows every stored row with
those values masked.

**F7. No guardrail check gets a caller's credential.** Every check gets a copy
of the request's metadata without the key and without any entry whose name
reads as a credential by F6's rule (a caller's own dimension such
as `session_token`, `access_token_2` or `tenant_key` included; one that
counts tokens, such as `token_budget` or `token_usage`, is kept): the
gateway config file's rules and an organization's checks, before and after
the provider, and the streaming checker's. A webhook still receives the
request, the client's address, the key's id, name and owner and the
request's other dimensions. An async job
keeps no credential in its metadata either; its run holds the key in
memory until it has run, to authenticate it again.

**F8. A native Google API call sends Google the organization's key.** On
`/v1beta`, through a provider with the native Google format, the gateway
forwards no client credential header: the provider sends its own key on
`generateContent`, `streamGenerateContent`, `countTokens` and `embedContent`,
and the client's other `X-Goog-*` headers (`X-Goog-Api-Client` and the like)
are forwarded as before.

**F9. Only an organization's Owner or Admin can change its guardrail
policies.** A guardrail policy's checks are merged into the organization's
config and pushed to the gateway, so anyone but an Owner or Admin gets 403 on
`/agentcc/guardrail-policies/` writes, as for every other change to the
gateway's config, and nothing changes. Every member can still list and read
them. The console does not use these endpoints.

**F10. A guardrail webhook, external check or FutureAGI eval check an
organization configures cannot reach the operator's internal network.** A
webhook guardrail (its `url`), an external-provider guardrail (its `endpoint`,
`base_url` or `region`) and a FutureAGI eval guardrail (its `base_url`) dial
through the gateway's guarded egress path, the same one MCP upstreams
use: a loopback, private, link-local or cloud-metadata address is refused
after DNS, on every attempt, and every redirect hop is re-checked; a redirect
to another origin carries none of the check's keys. A FutureAGI eval check
that names no `base_url`, the built-in default, or exactly the gateway's
`FI_BASE_URL` goes to `FI_BASE_URL`, the operator's own evaluation backend,
as before, with no listing (U12). A self-hosted
deployment reaches a guardrail service on a private or LAN address only when
the operator lists it in `mcp.egress.private_allow`; a check that names a
refused address returns a call failure, so the request is handled under the
check's action (refused when it blocks). The gateway's own `config.yaml`
guardrail rules are operator-trusted and keep a direct client, so an
operator check on a private host keeps working without being listed. A
guardrail that names a non-http(s) URL, a URL with no host, a URL carrying a
user or password, or one with a control character is refused when it is
saved, naming the field.

**F11. An organization's alert channel cannot reach the operator's internal
network.** An org's alert channel (a webhook or Slack URL) dials through the
same guarded egress path, with the same `mcp.egress.private_allow` exception
for a self-hosted LAN collector. The operator's own `config.yaml` alert
channels keep a direct client. A channel URL that is not a well-formed http(s)
URL is refused when it is saved, naming the field.

**F12. An org-config infrastructure secret is never shown to a reader.** The
cache backends' Redis password, S3 secret access key, Azure connection string
and SAS token and the semantic cache's vector-store API keys, the A2A agents'
tokens, the audit-sink and alert-channel webhook headers, and the MCP servers'
auth are stored encrypted and returned as a mask with a `secrets_set` flag,
and decrypted only for the push to the gateway, which still receives the real
values. A secret is write-only: a save that leaves it blank, or sends back the
mask, keeps the stored value; the console's secret field offers Replace and
Remove (Remove sends null and deletes it). A kept secret goes only to an
origin (scheme, host and port) it is already stored for: a save that moves its
host to another origin is refused `secret_origin_changed` and the secret must
be entered again. A read-back mask keeps the secret stored for that leaf's
origin, so one value stored for two hosts (a `Content-Type` header on two
alert channels, one MCP token for two servers) saves at each. A URL the
platform cannot read (it does not parse) names no origin: a secret is kept
there only while that URL is left exactly as stored. Every spelling the
gateway contract accepts for a secret field is a secret too:
`secretAccessKey`, `connectionString`, `sasToken` and `apiKey` as well as the
snake_case names, and the `azureBlob` and `managedTools` sections as well as
`azure_blob` and `managed_tools`. Provider keys and guardrail-check
credentials are encrypted at rest too. Migrations 0047 and 0050 encrypt
every stored row (U13).

**F13. An MCP server secret cannot be set through `POST /agentcc/org-configs/`.**
That endpoint imports a whole config version; an MCP server credential
