(`auth.token`, `auth.key`, `credential.secret` or a managed-tool secret) sent
there as a new clear value is refused `secret_in_config`, as the MCP
credential vault refuses one. `update-mcp-server` saves an MCP server's
credential write-only; a config with no new MCP secret, or one that keeps a
stored one, saves.

**F14. Removing an organization's last guardrail policy switches its checks
off.** Deactivating or deleting the last guardrail policy that holds a check
pushes an empty guardrails section, the gateway stops running those checks,
and later saves go ahead. A rules editor save with every rule removed is
pushed the same way.

**F15. The Guardrails page's switch and edit work on guardrails a policy
wrote.** When a guardrail policy sync last wrote an organization's guardrails
(a checks list), the on/off switch (toggle-guardrail) and the edit
(update-guardrail) change the check in the policy it came from, or, for a
check no policy holds, in the console's own policy, so the gateway runs the
change and later policy syncs keep it. A new credential typed in the edit is
stored encrypted in that policy. The same refusals apply as on the other
shapes (U10): a check whose stored credential is a `${VAR}` can be switched
off, not on. A checks list no policy wrote is changed as a list.

**F16. A guardrail policy's check needs a name.** A policy check whose name
is not a string (a number, a list, an object, null) or is empty is refused:
creating or changing such a policy answers 400 naming `checks[i].name`, and
nothing is saved.

**F17. A change to another organization's guardrail policy answers 404.** A
`PATCH`, `PUT`, `DELETE` or `apply` of a guardrail policy id another
organization owns, or of an unknown id, answers 404, as reading it does; the
other organization's policy is never changed, decrypted or pushed.

**F18. Only an organization's Owner or Admin can change its shadow
experiments.** A shadow experiment mirrors a share of the organization's
traffic to another model and provider, and pausing, resuming or completing one
pushes the organization's config, so anyone but an Owner or Admin gets 403 on
`/agentcc/shadow-experiments/` writes, as for every other change to the
gateway's config (F9), and nothing changes. Every member can still list them,
read one and its stats. The console's Experiments page shows these actions
disabled, with "Owner or Admin only", to anyone but an Owner or Admin (F23).

**F19. An organization's stored response is kept under its own id.**
Stored `/v1/responses` are keyed by organization and id: the same id is
stored, read, deleted and expired separately for each organization, and no
organization can read, replace or delete another's.

**F20. Prometheus label values are escaped.** `/-/metrics` writes label values
escaped as the Prometheus text format defines (backslash, double quote, line
feed), so each label set is its own series. `/-/metrics` needs the
admin token and is served only when `prometheus.enabled` is set.

**F21. Model-hub provider keys and secrets are stored encrypted.** The model
hub's provider keys (Settings, API keys, text and JSON keys), custom-model
credentials and the secrets a dataset's vector-database and API-call columns
use are stored as Fernet ciphertext under `INTEGRATION_ENCRYPTION_KEY`, the
way the gateway's provider credentials and F12's secrets are. The server
decrypts a key only to use it (a provider call, a dataset column, a Hugging
Face load) and to build its mask. Every response shows the same mask as
before, never the key or its ciphertext. A key this deployment
cannot decrypt is listed as "cannot be decrypted, enter this key again", and a
call that needs it fails with an error naming the key and
`INTEGRATION_ENCRYPTION_KEY`; no other key, FutureAGI's own included, is used
in its place. A stored ciphertext typed into a key field is stored as typed
and treated as a key that cannot be decrypted; it never decrypts to the key it
came from. Migration `model_hub` 0125 encrypts stored keys (U15). Without a
valid `INTEGRATION_ENCRYPTION_KEY` the server logs a warning that names the
variable and the fix (U15).

**F22. An alert channel URL that carries a credential is never shown to a
reader.** A Slack incoming-webhook URL is its channel's credential, and so
is a webhook URL with a token in its path or query. Such a URL is stored
encrypted, returned as a mask with a `secrets_set` flag on every org-config
read (Settings > Full Config, `org-configs/`, `active/`, the diff and the
write answers), and decrypted only for the push and the gateway's bulk sync,
which still receive the real URL. Which URLs count: a `slack` channel's URL;
any channel URL that does not parse, or has a user or password, a query or a
fragment; and any URL with a path segment that is not a short lowercase word
(`alerts`, `hooks`, `on-call` stay readable; a digit, an upper-case letter,
a `%` or more than 24 characters make it secret). Such a URL is write-only:
a blank or the read-back mask keeps it, a new URL replaces it, and null
removes it. A blank keeps the channel's own URL even after an earlier
channel was removed, and two channels that share a name each keep their own.
Another secret's mask is not accepted as a URL, nor a URL's mask as another
secret (refused with 400). Migration 0051 encrypts every stored URL of this
kind (U13). In the console, the Alerting tab shows a stored one as
dots with Replace, and Monitoring says it is stored and hidden.

**F23. The console disables what only an Owner or Admin may change.** The
platform refuses anyone but an organization's Owner or Admin every change to
the gateway's config, its API keys and its shadow experiments (F9, F18). The
console now shows those controls disabled, with "Owner or Admin only", to a
Member, a Viewer or a user whose role has not loaded: Add Provider and a
provider's Edit and Remove; API keys' Sync, Create Key, Edit and Revoke Key;
shadow experiments' New, Pause, Resume, Complete and Delete; Activate in the
config version history; the managed-models switch; the Guardrails page's
switch, Edit, Save & Activate and Run Test; the Org
Config editor's Save & Activate; Budgets' Set Budget, Remove and the
Enforce budgets switch; Settings > Batch Jobs' Submit Batch Job and a
running batch's Cancel (F41); Fallbacks' Save & Apply and Providers >
Routing's Save Routing Config; Monitoring's Create Rule, Add Channel and
each rule's and channel's Delete. Auto routing
is read-only for them too (its own note says edit access is
needed). Every read stays open. Add Provider follows the organization role.

**F24. A provider URL whose address carries a loopback or metadata IPv4
address in an IPv6 form is refused.** The platform's address check judges a
NAT64 (`64:ff9b::/96`), 6to4 (`2002::/16`), IPv4-compatible (`::a.b.c.d`) or
Teredo (`2001::/32`) address by the stricter of its own class and the IPv4
address it carries, and GCP's IPv6 metadata address `fd20:ce::254` is never
allowed. So a provider `base_url` whose address carries a loopback or
cloud-metadata address is refused even with
`AGENTCC_ALLOW_PRIVATE_PROVIDER_URLS=true`; one that carries a private
address saves with that setting.

**F25. Provider blocks in `config.example.yaml` start as written.** The
Perplexity, azure, azure-ad, groq, mistral, together, huggingface, fireworks,
deepinfra, cerebras, xai and openrouter blocks each state `base_url` and
`api_format`; Perplexity also states `api_path_prefix: ""`, so chat goes to
`https://api.perplexity.ai/chat/completions`.

**F26. An IPv6 address that carries an IPv4 address is checked as that
IPv4 address.** The egress guard (org guardrail callbacks and alert channels),
the provider `base_url` guard and Bedrock's dialer refuse a NAT64
(`64:ff9b::/96`), 6to4 (`2002::/16`), IPv4-compatible (`::a.b.c.d`) or Teredo
(`2001::/32`) address as they refuse the IPv4 address in it.
An IPv4 range in `mcp.egress.private_allow` opens only the IPv4 form of an
address: list the name or the IPv6 range.

**F27. Set Budget turns budgets on.** A budget saved from Budgets > Set Budget
turns the organization's budgets on when any level has a limit above 0 and the
organization never chose; an explicit off stays off; removing the last level
turns them off. The Budgets page shows the same switch, "Enforce budgets", as
Org Config > Budgets. From the gateway's `org_sections_enforced`, the page
says "Not enforced on this gateway" when that gateway does not run the budget
plugin, and "Enforcement unknown" when the gateway does not report it.

**F28. Throttle admits a set rate past the limit.** Past the limit, the budget
admits its "Requests per minute" (`throttle_rpm`, a whole number from 1,
default 1) in each clock minute on each gateway replica, as the console says,
and refuses the rest with 429 `budget_throttled` and `Retry-After` (the
seconds to the next minute); under the limit every request is admitted. A
request that another level refuses takes no throttle slot. `throttle_rpm` is a
new field of a budget level and of the organization budget in the admin
contract.

**F29. The Alert Threshold reaches the gateway.** Set Budget's Alert
Threshold (%) is the budget's `warn_threshold` (on a team, user, key or tag
budget, its `alert_thresholds`): from that share of the limit, each
request's log carries `org_budget_warning`. The dialog takes a percentage
above 0 and up to 100; a stored value outside that range is not sent.

**F30. The Organization or Hard Limit budget saved last is enforced.**
With both set, each save carries the time it was made (`saved_at`), the one
saved last is enforced whole, and the Budgets page marks the other "Not in
force".

**F31. Budget bars show real spend.** Each organization-level bar shows the
organization's spend for that budget's period from the request logs:
`GET /agentcc/spend-summary/` answers a signed-in console user with their own
organization only (the gateway's admin token reads every organization). Team,
user, key and tag budgets are listed one per entry; their spend is counted by
the gateway and is not shown on the page. Roles & Access says how the gateway
identifies a request.

**F32. Saving an organization's config keeps its budgets' spend.** The gateway
keeps each organization's budget counters across config saves, also when the
config is removed; each resets at the start of its period.

**F33. A budget set partway through a period counts the period's spend so
far.** The gateway counts every organization's spend at the organization
level, whether its budgets are on, off or not set, and each team, user, key
and tag budget an organization configures, on or off. The Budgets page says
that the gateway checks each limit against its own count (see Known limits).
Set Budget's limit field names the period the gateway applies: the
organization's Budget Period in Org Config > Budgets.

**F34. Email alerts are sent.** Each active email alert is sent through its
own provider (SMTP, SendGrid or Resend). Error Occurred, Guardrail Triggered
and Budget Exceeded are recorded when the gateway's request log reaches the
platform, and the email is started on Temporal, never on the request that
delivered the log. Error Rate Spike, Latency Spike and Cost Threshold are
checked every minute against the organization's own request logs over the
alert's window (`window_minutes`, default 5): the error rate and the p95
latency only with at least `min_requests` requests (default 5), against
`error_rate_percent` (default 5) and `latency_ms` (default 5000); spend
against `cost_usd` (default 10). Each alert sends at most one email per
`cooldown_minutes`; events that arrive inside the cooldown are sent
together in the next email, with how often each happened. A threshold that
stays crossed is sent again after each cooldown. A failed send is recorded
on the alert (`last_error`, shown on the Email Alerts card), logged with the
alert's id, kept and retried on the next minute's check, or sooner when the
organization's next request event is recorded; it never fails the log
delivery.

**F35. Budget Exceeded is produced.** Webhooks and email alerts get Budget
Exceeded: a request the gateway refuses for a budget (error code
`budget_exceeded`, `org_budget_exceeded` or `budget_throttled`, from the
gateway's own error, never from the caller's metadata) produces it, once per
organization per budget level per budget period (the refusal's
`budget_exceeded_level` and `budget_period_start`),
or, when the refusal names no level, once per error code per UTC day.

**F36. Webhook retries run.** A schedule delivers due webhook events every
30 s, waiting 30 s after the first failed attempt and doubling the wait after
each, up to the event's `max_attempts` (5); Retry starts the delivery at
once, off the request. One event is never sent twice by two workers at the
same time.

**F37. Analytics charts are bucketed at the range's size.** The console
asks for 5-minute buckets up to 6 h, 15-minute up to 24 h, hourly up to 7
days, 6-hour up to 30 days and daily beyond, and gets each at that size (C59).

**F38. Retry, failover and circuit-breaker status lists match the
provider's own status.** A provider adapter answers a provider's 5xx as 502,
and a list matches the status the client gets or the provider's own (kept
beside the 502 for OpenAI-compatible and Azure providers and on every unparsed
error; read from the error code for Cohere, Bedrock, Anthropic and Gemini), so
a list that names 502 matches every provider 5xx. "Retry on Timeout"
(`routing.retry.on_timeout`) and failover's `on_timeout` act on a provider
timeout (504 `gateway_timeout`, or a provider's 408 or 504). A circuit breaker
counts a provider timeout as a failure whatever its `on_status_codes`.

**F39. The response cache uses TLS when `tls` is set.** With
`cache.redis.tls: true` every connection to Redis is TLS or fails (U19).

**F40. Console batch jobs run as your organization.** Settings > Batch Jobs
mints a key for your organization for each batch (named `console-batch-<id>`,
listed with your API keys, owned by the user who submitted it, expiring 24 h
after it was made) and the gateway runs every item as that key (C67). The
key is revoked as soon as the batch is seen completed, failed or cancelled,
and in any case 24 h after the batch was submitted. A batch's status and
results are read and cancelled only for your organization. Console batch
jobs need a single gateway replica (C67; the Helm chart runs one by
default): with several, a submit can be refused and a batch's status can
answer "Gateway error ... 404". Each item's client address is the console
server's, so an organization IP allowlist (on a gateway that enforces it,
U16) must include that address; otherwise every item of a console batch
answers 403.

**F41. Only an organization's Owner or Admin can submit or cancel a batch
job.** Submitting or cancelling a console batch spends the organization's
credits on its providers, so anyone but an Owner or Admin gets 403 and nothing
reaches the gateway. Every member can follow a batch's status and results, and
Check Health stays open to every member.

**F42. Webhooks subscribed to Batch Completed receive it.** The first time the
console or the `agentcc-batches` schedule (U20) sees a batch
completed, failed or cancelled, every active webhook of the organization
subscribed to `batch.completed` receives one event, once per batch:
`{"event": "batch.completed", "batch_id", "org_id", "status", "total",
"created_at", "completed_at", "summary"}`, where `summary` is the gateway's
(`total_cost`, `total_input_tokens`, `total_output_tokens`, `completed`,
`failed`, `cancelled`). `status` is the gateway's `completed` or
`cancelled`, or `failed` for a batch the gateway does not hold (it
restarted, or the finished batch's hour passed) 24 h after it was
submitted; that event has no `summary`, and adds `"outcome": "unknown"` and
an `error` saying so. Delivery and retries are the webhook's usual ones.
The console shows such a batch as failed, and stops polling it, as soon as
its page reads it after those 24 h; a finished batch whose results the
gateway does not hold shows the status it finished with, without results.

**F43. An organization's alert rule waits its cooldown.** After a rule fires,
it waits its cooldown before it can fire again, while its condition still
holds. A rule saved without a cooldown, or with one that is not a duration,
waits 15 minutes. `0s` is honoured: the rule fires on every request past its
threshold. A config push (a save in any section) keeps the cooldown of a rule
whose name is unchanged. Monitoring > Create Rule has a Cooldown field (it
starts at 15m), and the Alert Rules table shows each rule's cooldown, or "15m
(default)".

**F44. A Monitoring alert rule says "Active" only when this gateway runs
it.** The Status chip reads which org config sections the gateway reports it
enforces (`org_sections_enforced` in `GET /-/config`): Active when that list
includes `alerting`; "Not enforced on this gateway" when it does not;
"Enforcement unknown" when the gateway does not report the list; "Alerting
off" while the organization's alerting switch is off.

**F45. Monitoring's Live Metrics cards open the Analytics tab they name.**
Each card opens its own tab (Usage, Errors, Latency, Cost). The request card
is labelled "Requests (24h)", the count it shows.

**F46. "Gateway Connected" is ticked only when the gateway is healthy.**
The Overview's Getting Started step is ticked only when the platform's last
health check of the gateway succeeded. While the gateway is not healthy, the
Overview offers Check Health to every member, and the check reports its real
result ("Gateway healthy", or "Gateway unreachable" with the reason).

**F47. Settings shows the gateway's own settings.** The Settings cards show
the gateway's read, write, idle and maximum request
times, its request logging, body logging and content mode, the
organization's last health check with its outcome, and the org sections
this gateway enforces (C70, C71); a value the gateway does not report
reads "Not reported". The Batch API card says batches live in the gateway
node's memory (a restart loses them), that concurrency is set per
submission (10 when a submission sets none) and that a submission may be up
to 10 MiB. Shutdown Timeout and the log format, which no gateway reports,
are not shown.

**F48. Provider Health shows each provider's own health.** The table says how
the status is derived (C69), shows why each row has its status, labels latency
as the 24-hour average and has no circuit breaker column. A provider the
config push leaves out reads "Not pushed" with the reason. Org Config >
Providers links to Providers > Provider Config, where each provider's Edit
dialog is, and shows each provider's status and reason.

**F49. The Guardrails Test tab says what happened.** The tab shows Blocked
(403); Warned or Logged, with the check names when the gateway sends them;
Passed; or Error with the HTTP status and the gateway's message, in the result
and in Recent Tests. Its help text names 403 `guardrail_error` and the
`x-agentcc-guardrail-*` headers. The Guardrails Logs
tab colours each request by the strongest action its checks took (block,
warn, log).

**F50. A malformed URL stored under a name two entries share does not lock a
save out.** The checks that refuse a save introducing a malformed alert
channel or guardrail callback URL let a URL stand when the same name already
holds it unchanged, and each name holds every URL stored under it, so two
alert channels or two guardrail checks of the same name each keep their stored
URL. A new malformed URL under a shared name is refused, naming its own field,
and another name never vouches for one.

**F51. An organization keeps its last good config when the platform cannot
build a new one.** When the platform cannot build one organization's
config for the gateway's sync, it still serves every other organization's
and names that one in `unbuilt_org_ids`; the gateway keeps the config it
already holds for each organization named there. An organization that is
neither served nor named is dropped.

### Present but switched off

- **Managed models and credits.** The code to serve models with FutureAGI's
  own upstream keys, hold and settle prepaid credit, and answer 402 is in
  this release, but nothing turns it on. No shipped config marks a provider
  `managed: true`. Without one, the managed admin routes answer 404
  `managed_models_disabled`, and the console offers no managed models (N7).
  Do not mark a provider `managed: true` in this release: settled
  managed spend does not reach the billing ledger yet.
- **Managed tools.** `tools.enabled` defaults to false, and a managed tool
  call answers 503 `managed_capability_unavailable`.
- **Settings that nothing reads.** `security.*`,
  `telemetry.overhead_header`, `responses.hollow.*`, `runtime_tokens.*` and
  `managed.upstream_key_mode` are parsed and have no effect.
- **Audit sinks other than stdout, and `cluster.redis_url`.** The audit log
  is written to stdout only (JSON lines; ship them to a SIEM with your log
  collector). An `audit.sinks` entry of type `file` or `webhook` is
  accepted and writes nothing.
  Cluster membership is per node (`GET /-/cluster/nodes` lists the node
  that answers), and `cluster.redis_url` is not read; replicas share rate
  limits, budgets, credits, key revocations and key rotations through the
  `redis` block instead. The gateway starts with either set and names each
  in one startup warning.

### Known limits

- **Release signing.** The release workflow adds an SBOM, checksums, a
  keyless signature and a SLSA provenance attestation to each release it
  builds; no published release carries them
  ([supply-chain.md](security/supply-chain.md#what-the-release-workflow-does-ci-only)).
- **Span bodies follow `otel.include_bodies`.** Content mode (N4) governs
  the request log; leave `otel.include_bodies` off where an organization or
  key uses content mode `metadata` or `none`.
- **Where a check on the answer reads.** It reads the answer's text on the
  five routes in C30, as C25 and C26 say. On a stream it reads the answer
  when the stream ends. A streamed answer that a blocking check reads is
  held until the check passes, so it starts when the answer is complete
  (C25); tool-call frames in a held stream are not read by the checks. A
  check that does not block holds nothing. The streaming checker, which
  reads a chat stream as it goes, holds the stream too in stop mode, and
  stops the provider's stream as soon as a rule blocks. On the APIs whose
  answer is not model text (C35) there is no answer to withhold.
- **What a held stream costs.** Until its checks have run, a held stream
  (C25) keeps up to 10 MiB of the response's frames and up to 10 MiB of the
  answer assembled for the checks; a stream past either is refused with
  `answer_unavailable`. Size the gateway's memory for the long answers it
  holds at once. Nothing is sent while a stream is held, keep-alive
  comments included, so a client's idle-read timeout needs to cover the
  time a long answer takes.
- **Where a check on the request reads.** It reads the request's text on
  `/v1/chat/completions`, `/v1/completions` and the routes in C32 and C35
  (the console's Guardrails page lists them), and on async, batch and A2A
  requests, which are chat requests; a batch item runs as the batch key's
  organization (C67). A check that would block or rewrite a request refuses
  what it cannot read: the input of a transcription, a translation or OCR
  (C35), and a value nested deeper than the gateway reads (C36). A rewrite
  that cannot be written back refuses the request rather than send it (C33).
- **`mask` applies to `pii-detection`** (C34). The console offers `mask`
  only for `pii-detection`; set a check of any other type to block, warn or
  log. On a self-hosted gateway, give an organization's check a name that no
  rule in the config file uses.
- **An organization's other checks read the request only.** PII, content
  moderation, secrets, keyword blocklist, validation, FutureAGI and
  external provider checks set in an organization's config never read the
  answer.
- **Send credentials only in credential headers.** The request log masks
  every header whose name reads as a credential (F6), and a guardrail
  check's input leaves out such a dimension (F7). Keep secrets out of other
  headers and out of metadata dimensions, which the request log keeps
  (content mode `none` drops them).
- **A guardrail webhook receives the request.** A webhook check still sends
  the URL its config names the request, the client's address, the key's
  id, name and owner and the request's other dimensions (F7). Through the
  platform, only an organization's Owner or Admin can set that URL (F9).
- **The gateway checks a callback's address when it dials.** On save the
  platform refuses a guardrail or alert URL that is not a well-formed http(s)
  URL with a host and no user, password or control character (F10, F11).
  What the URL may connect to is decided at dial time by the gateway's egress
  guard against `mcp.egress.private_allow`, whatever way the check reached
  the gateway (F10, F11, U12).
- **The gateway admin token is operator scope.** The gateway's admin API
  (`/-/`) serves every organization's config to whoever holds the admin
  token: keep that token to the people who run the gateway.
- **Alert URLs are classed as secret by their shape** (F22): a URL the rule
  takes for a secret is masked, and still delivered.
- **Browsers send only three agent headers by default**: `x-agentcc-user-id`,
  `x-agentcc-session-id` and `x-agentcc-trace-id`; the others need
  `cors.allowed_headers` (C7).
- **No async for organization keys** (C16).
- **What `model: "auto"` does not do** (N2): no benchmark shows that it picks
  better models than a fixed choice. Do not rely on it to steer away from a
  failing provider. Its ranking also weighs the latency and errors this
  gateway has recorded for each provider name.
- **`routing.mirror` in the config file applies to organization keys too.**
  On a self-hosted gateway, leave it unset where organizations' traffic must
  stay on their own providers.
- **An IP rule is only as reliable as the client address** (U3, C17).
- **The platform prices a log entry with no gateway cost from the
  gateway's own usage fields** (C40), which request metadata cannot set.
- **`/v1/responses` is priced from the usage its answer reports** (F3). F3
  says how a prepaid stream whose last event cannot be read is charged and
  why a background response on prepaid credit is refused.
- **Per-key limits for platform keys come from the organization** (C22).
  The gateway reads only `log_content` from a key made through the
  platform; the organization's `per_key_rpm` and `per_key_tpm` (C66) apply
  to every key of the organization. On a key defined in the gateway's own
  config file (`auth.keys`), `rate_limit_rpm`, `rate_limit_tpm` and
  `allowed_ips` apply, and `rate_limit_tpm` is counted as C75 says.
- **What a token limit counts** (C66): the tokens of the response that
  served the request, the usage it is priced on. Without Redis each replica
  counts on its own.
- **Which routes an organization's rate limits count** (C66): chat
  completions, completions, Responses, Anthropic Messages and Gemini
  (`/v1beta`) requests, and the relayed speech sessions. `per_user_rpm`
  applies where the request names an end user (C66).
- **Rate limits count the requests the provider serves** (C66); an answer
  from the response cache is not counted.
- **A budget's throttle is counted on each gateway replica** (F28). The
  spend the limit is checked against is shared through Redis when the
  gateway has one.
- **The gateway checks a budget against its own count of the spend.** The
  Budgets page's bars read the request logs; the gateway counts spend as it
  answers (F33). With more than one replica, keep `redis` on (U16).
- **An alert rule's cooldown is kept per gateway replica** (F43), in its
  memory: a restart starts it afresh.
- **Chat parameters the Anthropic, Gemini and Bedrock adapters do not send.**
  A chat request translated for one of these providers loses these fields,
  with no error or header to say so: on Anthropic and Bedrock, `n`, `seed`,
  `presence_penalty`, `frequency_penalty`, `logit_bias`, `logprobs`,
  `top_logprobs`, `user`, `service_tier`, a `response_format` of type
  `json_object` (only `json_schema` is sent) and a request for extended
  thinking (`thinking`, `thinking_budget` or `reasoning_effort`); on Gemini,
  `n`, `seed`, `presence_penalty`, `frequency_penalty`, `logit_bias`,
  `logprobs`, `top_logprobs`, `user` and `service_tier` (`json_object`,
  `json_schema`, `thinking_budget` and `reasoning_effort` are sent).
  Requests to OpenAI-format providers carry them as sent
  (`TestChatParametersThisAdapterDoesNotSend` in each of the three
  adapters; `TestJSONObjectAndThinkingThisAdapterDoesNotSend` for
  Anthropic and Bedrock, `TestJSONFormatsAndThinkingThisAdapterSends` for
  Gemini).

### Proof

Go tests run with `go test ./...` in `agentcc-gateway`. `sc/NN` is a
scenario script in `agentcc-gateway/scripts/scenarios/`, and a bare `.sh`
name is a gate script in `scripts/scenarios/gates/`; both run the real gateway
binary against a mock upstream and need Redis (`make scenarios`). Django
tests are in `futureagi/agentcc/tests/`, and frontend tests sit next to
their components in `frontend/src/sections/gateway/`.
